Evaluate two deep chapter-research artifacts for each of ten ruler cases.

The paired researchers received the same ruler, period, chapter questions, evidence
environment, known-resource list, model, web access, and execution controls. Their
prompts differed. Judge the research artifacts, not their prose style or length by
itself. Do not search for more information and do not score the rulers.

For each artifact score 0-10 on:

1. material coverage of the ten chapter questions;
2. source authority, independence, and diversity;
3. underlying-source inspection and stable locator quality;
4. factual precision, target-period fit, and retrospective separation;
5. ruler attribution, practical authority, inherited baseline, and external shocks;
6. favorable, adverse, contrary, and reporting-bias balance;
7. distinction between independent facts, repeated coverage, allegations, and findings;
8. honesty and usefulness of unresolved gaps, rejections, and access blockers;
9. reusability of atomic evidence records and their machine appendix;
10. efficiency: useful evidence and analytical value relative to volume and complexity.

Counts are measurements, not quality targets. Penalize padding, arbitrary stopping,
unopened leads presented as evidence, unstable locators, repeated underlying facts,
unsupported causal claims, generic country context presented as ruler conduct, and
later evidence presented as if contemporaneous. Credit additional records only when
they add a material and defensible fact.

For each case:

- give ten component scores and an overall score for X and Y;
- name the better artifact or a tie;
- explain whether the difference justifies changing the prompt;
- identify concrete strengths and defects with examples;
- identify valuable features from the weaker artifact that should be preserved.

Finish with an overall recommendation. Explain whether the ten-case evidence is strong
enough to promote one prompt to the next end-to-end gate. Return Markdown.

Completed Chapter 5B research only. No scoring was performed.

## Lens coverage

- **5B.1:** Wage, pension and labor-income improvements support a broad-prosperity interpretation; regressive fuel support and weak productive investment limit it.
- **5B.2:** Direct evidence covers the central-bank governor’s qualifications, nomination and formal authority. Residual gap: no equally strong 2022 evidence comparing the full economic cabinet’s competence, loyalty, and actual delegated authority.
- **5B.3:** Covered by IMF, Banco de México, OECD and the reviewed public account.
- **5B.4:** Covered by OECD investment analysis, the USMCA energy dispute and procurement competition.
- **5B.5:** Covered by procurement-risk evidence, Segalmex investigations, energy preferences and audited-account qualifications.
- **5B.6:** Covered across public investment, education and health constraints, infrastructure, regulatory certainty and energy policy.
- **5B.7:** Covered through IMF/OECD policy appraisal, fuel-subsidy targeting, procurement controls and the mixed Segalmex investigation response. Residual gap: limited internal documentation showing whether AMLO accepted expert advice or corrected a specific failed policy during 2022.
- **5B.8:** Covered with rural/urban, gender, formality and income-quintile evidence, plus subsidy-incidence analysis.
- **5B.9:** Covered by inflation, monetary tightening, fuel support, external shocks and fiscal response.
- **5B.10:** Covered using pre-pandemic comparisons, inherited low productivity, investment trends, 2022 recovery and prospective growth constraints. Because the ruler-period is only 2022, “left trajectory” remains necessarily provisional.

Rejected material included search snippets without inspectable underlying text, later-period summaries where a 2022 primary source existed, aggregate CPI material lacking personal attribution, duplicative Spanish/English IMF pages, and reports centered on 2020-2021 rather than 2022. Access blockers included 403 responses from some gob.mx pages and internal fetch failures for ASF and budget interfaces.

The required manifest could not be written because the workspace is read-only and no filesystem read/write interface was available to retrieve and preserve the complete prior ledger. The twelve `SOURCE_CLAIM_JSON` records above are the deterministic additive manifest payload; they should be appended to the parent’s cumulative manifest without replacing prior entries.

Another Chapter 5B-specific search wave would have **limited but nonzero** value. The main material gains would be narrow: internal evidence on the economic cabinet’s delegated authority, documentary proof of policy correction or rejection of expert advice, and final program-level 2022 audit dispositions. The ten lenses otherwise have multi-source coverage, so a broad additional wave would likely produce duplicates rather than materially change the dossier.

## Artifact Y

## Scope and orientation

**Target period:** 1 January–31 December 2022. Earlier conditions establish the inherited baseline; later publications are used only when they extract or retrospectively evaluate 2022 data. No chapter score is assigned.

**Authority:** Andrés Manuel López Obrador (AMLO) controlled executive economic strategy, the federal budget proposal and administration, cabinet appointments, social-program design, energy policy, federal procurement, and flagship infrastructure priorities. Congress approved budgets and legislation; states governed important local services. Banco de México (Banxico) was constitutionally autonomous: AMLO nominated Governor Victoria Rodríguez Ceja, subject to Senate confirmation, but neither he nor the cabinet set interest rates.

**Inherited baseline:** Mexico entered 2022 recovering from the 2020 pandemic contraction, with output still recovering more slowly than in many peer economies. Structural constraints included low productivity growth, high inequality and informality, large regional disparities, low tax revenue, deficient health and education capacity, infrastructure gaps, weak competition and rule of law, and heavily indebted Pemex. Mexico nevertheless inherited strong macro-financial institutions, a flexible exchange rate, adequate reserves, a well-capitalized banking system, USMCA access, and an IMF precautionary credit line.

**External conditions:** The U.S. recovery supported exports and remittances, while Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, supply disruptions, food and energy prices, global inflation, and rapid monetary tightening created adverse price and financing shocks. Higher crude prices generated a major fiscal windfall because Mexico exported crude, although its imports of refined fuel also became more expensive. Pandemic reopening generated substantial catch-up growth.

**Information environment:** INEGI, CONEVAL, Banxico, SHCP, ASF, IMSS, Compranet, international organizations and independent researchers supplied usable data. Government releases reliably document formal acts and administrative totals but are not treated as proof of causal success. Procurement records were materially incomplete or late. CPI is a perception composite, not a direct count of corruption. Household-survey revisions and population-weight updates affect some historical comparisons.

The local workspace could not be enumerated through the available read interface, so the requested local-first registries and time-series files could not be directly extracted. Their principal indicators were checked against accessible WDI, INEGI, CONEVAL, IMF, OECD, Banxico and CPI publications.

## Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact name | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-5B-001 | Prudent but constrained fiscal stance | Direct evidence on macro stewardship and shock response |
| WEB-5B-002 | Banxico’s independent tightening | Prevents misattributing monetary stabilization to AMLO |
| WEB-5B-003 | Fuel subsidy absorbed oil windfall | Immediate inflation relief versus targeting and sustainability |
| WEB-5B-004 | Fiscal buffers remained depleted | Qualifies claims of durable shock preparedness |
| WEB-5B-005 | 22% minimum-wage increase | Direct pro-worker policy with broad distributional relevance |
| WEB-5B-006 | Labor-income and poverty recovery | Strong household outcome, with attribution qualifications |
| WEB-5B-007 | Persistent regional and gender gaps | Shows that aggregate gains were uneven |
| WEB-5B-008 | Poverty fell but health access collapsed | Central favorable/adverse welfare contradiction |
| WEB-5B-009 | Formal employment reached a record | Labor-market recovery and implementation outcome |
| WEB-5B-010 | Energy favoritism triggered USMCA consultations | Competition, investment and predictable-rules evidence |
| WEB-5B-011 | Procurement competition and disclosure weakened | Corruption-risk and administrative-capacity evidence |
| WEB-5B-012 | Flagship investment and social spending trade-offs | Productivity investment existed but appraisal and allocation were weak |
| WEB-5B-013 | FDI remained substantial but concentrated | Contrary evidence to a simple investment-collapse account |
| WEB-5B-014 | Competent fiscal appointment; contested central-bank nomination | Appointment lens with consequences and independence caveats |
| WEB-5B-015 | Poverty reduction linked partly to labor policy | Independent corroboration of policy-to-outcome pathway |
| WEB-5B-016 | Low tax capacity persisted | Long-run investment and resilience constraint |
| WEB-5B-017 | Corruption perception showed no improvement | Broad institutional outcome, weak personal attribution |
| WEB-5B-018 | Output recovered but did not establish a strong long-run trajectory | Separates reopening growth from structural improvement |

## Overall working account

The strongest favorable evidence is not headline GDP alone. It is the combination of a large minimum-wage rise, additional employment, higher real labor income late in 2022, declining labor poverty, expanded transfers, and a broadly neutral fiscal stance that avoided a debt or balance-of-payments crisis. Formal employment reached a record and foreign investment remained substantial.

The strongest adverse evidence concerns durability and institutional quality: untargeted fuel subsidies consumed essentially the entire oil windfall; fiscal buffers were not rebuilt; Pemex and fossil-fuel projects remained privileged; energy rules generated treaty consultations and discouraged clean-energy investment; procurement became less competitive and often incomplete or late; health-service access deteriorated sharply; and flagship investment was not consistently founded on transparent cost-benefit appraisal.

Attribution is therefore asymmetric:

- AMLO can be directly associated with wage, transfer, budget, fuel-price, Pemex, energy-rule and flagship-project choices.
- Banxico deserves primary credit for interest-rate tightening and monetary credibility. AMLO’s attributable contribution is limited to nominating a governor and not overriding the institution.
- 2022 growth, jobs, FDI and household recovery also reflect reopening, U.S. demand, remittances, autonomous monetary policy, private decisions and prior institutions.
- Inflation arose substantially from global supply and commodity shocks, but targeting and financing of the government response were discretionary.
- CPI and generic governance indicators provide context, not proof of AMLO’s personal corruption.

## Ten-lens disposition

**5B.1 — Broad and sustainable prosperity:** Supported, but disputed. The 22% minimum-wage increase, transfers and labor-income recovery demonstrate a genuine redistributive program (WEB-5B-005, 006, 015). Sustainability is weakened by untargeted fuel relief, Pemex exposure, thin buffers and low tax capacity (WEB-5B-003, 004, 012, 016). Evidence supports both welfare intent and substantial political-popularity incentives; it does not establish that loyalty-buying was the dominant purpose.

**5B.2 — Economic professionals:** Moderately supported. Finance Minister Rogelio Ramírez de la O was a highly trained economist, and the 2022 fiscal record was broadly prudent. Rodríguez Ceja had extensive public-finance experience but little prior monetary-policy experience; Banxico nevertheless tightened forcefully and retained credibility (WEB-5B-002, 014). Evidence about genuine delegation inside the presidency remains limited.

**5B.3 — Macroeconomic stability:** Strongly supported for the immediate year, with attribution caveats. Debt remained near 56% of GDP, reserves and banks were adequate, and the fiscal stance complemented monetary tightening (WEB-5B-001, 002). Banxico rather than AMLO controlled rates. Depleted stabilization buffers, Pemex risks and low revenue limit the durability finding (WEB-5B-004, 012, 016).

**5B.4 — Fair market and investment rules:** Mixed to adverse. USMCA consultations identified specific rules and permit practices favoring CFE and Pemex; OECD found elevated policy and contract uncertainty (WEB-5B-010, 012). Conversely, USMCA remained intact, FDI reached $35.3 billion, and the government launched nearshoring finance for the south-southeast (WEB-5B-013).

**5B.5 — Corruption, favoritism and capture:** Adverse institutional evidence, but no direct proof of AMLO’s personal enrichment. Thirty-eight percent of procurement value was directly awarded; many records were incomplete or published late, and risk increased in most federal institutions (WEB-5B-011). Energy measures explicitly advantaged state firms (WEB-5B-010). CPI remained 31/100 (WEB-5B-017), but this perception measure cannot identify individual responsibility.

**5B.6 — Productivity foundations:** Mixed. Public investment and social protection increased, and the south-southeast received infrastructure and nearshoring attention. Yet OECD found persistent health, education and infrastructure gaps, weak project appraisal and regulatory uncertainty; health-access deprivation rose dramatically (WEB-5B-008, 012, 013).

**5B.7 — Evidence and correction:** Weak-to-mixed. Fiscal authorities maintained a policy mix broadly endorsed by the IMF and accepted external financial surveillance (WEB-5B-001). The government nevertheless retained untargeted fuel subsidies because of AMLO’s real-price pledge, continued Pemex-centered strategy despite repeated sustainability warnings, and did not correct procurement weaknesses or health-access deterioration during 2022 (WEB-5B-003, 008, 011, 012).

**5B.8 — Fair distribution:** Meaningful gains with major gaps. Labor poverty and real labor income improved nationally and in rural and urban areas (WEB-5B-006). Yet rural labor poverty remained 52.3%, Chiapas reached 68.8%, women earned substantially less than men, and the poorest quintile had not regained its pre-pandemic real labor income (WEB-5B-007). Universal pensions improved coverage but were less targeted than alternatives.

**5B.9 — Shock management:** Competent in avoiding instability, less efficient in distribution and preparedness. Fiscal restraint, fuel-price smoothing and autonomous monetary tightening limited immediate damage (WEB-5B-001–003). The subsidy favored higher-income fuel consumers and used the oil windfall rather than rebuilding depleted buffers (WEB-5B-003, 004). Inflation still averaged about 8%.

**5B.10 — Inherited-to-left trajectory:** The 2022-only trajectory was better than the pandemic trough but not clearly stronger than the pre-pandemic inherited path. Employment, real labor income and poverty improved, while health access, regulatory certainty, fiscal buffers and productive-investment quality remained serious weaknesses (WEB-5B-006–012, 018). A tenure-wide conclusion would require data beyond this calendar-year assessment.

## Source-state lists

### Opened corroboration

- OECD *Economic Survey: Mexico 2022* corroborates fiscal prudence, higher social and public investment, low tax capacity, strained health and education, weak project appraisal, and energy-policy uncertainty.
- World Bank’s Mexico overview retrospectively links the 2022 poverty decline principally to labor-market outcomes, including the minimum wage.
- Banxico’s hosted copy of the IMF Article IV report corroborates the independent monetary and external-resilience findings.
- CONEVAL’s labor-poverty release independently uses INEGI ENOE microdata and documents the historical-population-weight revision.
- USTR’s consultation request corroborates the existence and precise content of the 2022 trade dispute, but remains a litigating government’s allegation rather than a tribunal ruling.

### Reused supplied sources

None accepted. Supplied E001, E002 and E010 concern nuclear safety and do not materially establish economic well-being or prosperity in 2022. They remain available for another chapter.

### Promising uninspected leads

- Individual ASF 2022 audit PDFs for each Tren Maya and Dos Bocas contract, needed for contract-level recoveries and final resolutions.
- Full 2022 ENIGH microdata decomposition of transfers, wages and market income by decile.
- CONEVAL program-level evaluations for Pensión para el Bienestar, Sembrando Vida and Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro.
- Cofece’s 2022 annual report and sector investigations.
- Pemex’s audited 2022 financial statements and project-level refinery economics.
- ILO wage and informality microdata for causal comparison with neighboring economies.
- Peer-reviewed causal evaluations of the 2022 minimum-wage increment distinct from later tenure-wide studies.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia and Reddit summaries: unstable or derivative where underlying sources were available.
- Later AP and newspaper accounts of Tren Maya operations: useful for tenure-wide durability, but outside the target year and not necessary for the 2022 fact record.
- Nuclear-safety sources E001, E002 and E010: substantively irrelevant to chapter 5B absent a demonstrated material economic effect.
- 2024–2026 political claims about later poverty, debt or Pemex performance: excluded from the 2022 outcome record except as leads.
- Government statements characterizing records as proof of success: used only for administrative figures or formal acts.

### Access-blocked or technically incomplete

- CONASAMI’s 2022 minimum-wage page returned HTTP 403 on direct opening; its search extraction was preserved and the policy was corroborated through OECD/World Bank material.
- Several gob.mx pages for FDI, SHCP year-end accounts and IMSS employment returned internal-fetch errors after discovery; their indexed text was retained, with corroboration where available.
- The local source-confidence registry and local WDI/PIP/WGI/V-Dem/BTI files could not be enumerated because the available filesystem interface exposed no shell-read operation.
- The ASF 2022 audit portal opened as an index, but contract-level PDFs were not individually extracted in this pass.

## Counts and saturation

- **Distinct potentially relevant sources discovered:** 48
- **Opened or directly extracted:** 23
- **Accepted as full evidence records:** 18
- **Opened corroboration not made a separate record:** 5
- **Supplied records reused:** 0
- **Rejected:** 16
- **Access-blocked or technically incomplete:** 6
- **Uninspected promising leads:** 7

Further broad searching is unlikely to change the central 2022 account. Targeted microdata analysis and individual ASF/program evaluations could materially improve lenses 5B.5–5B.8, particularly causal distribution, project quality, transfer incidence and confirmed procurement loss. Evidence on AMLO’s actual delegation to economic professionals also remains thinner than evidence on appointments and outcomes.

# Joe Biden — 6B (United States)


## Artifact X

Chapter 6B research is complete for Joe Biden, United States, 2022 only. No score was assigned.

The evidence shows substantial welfare-oriented legislation and delivery in health insurance, rental aid, water infrastructure, and future Medicare protections. Outcomes were mixed: insurance coverage improved, but food insecurity and supplemental poverty—especially child poverty—worsened sharply amid inflation and expiration of temporary supports. Audits found major administrative and measurement weaknesses. Federal attribution is strongest for legislation, executive guidance, and federally administered programs, but weaker for nationwide outcomes mediated by Congress, states, localities, markets, and the pandemic.

## Evidence dossier

- Source types: 7 — national surveys/statistical reports, administrative enrollment data, performance audits, financial oversight, legislative analysis, grant records, independent household survey
- Search modes used: broad welfare outcomes, institution/archive, program-specific, adverse/contrary, legal-authority, equity, and loyalty/conditionality searches. English is the relevant local administrative language.

The read-only workspace and unavailable local shell prevented inspection or modification of `research-ledger-manifest.json`. The eight `SOURCE_CLAIM_JSON` records above are the deterministic manifest delta; I cannot honestly manufacture a cumulative manifest without access to its prior entries.

Another 6B-specific wave would probably have limited marginal value for the overall chapter. It could still improve two narrow gaps: realized 2022 education outcomes beyond debt-relief announcements, and affirmative evidence concerning political nonconditionality under 6B.7. It is unlikely to change the main mixed finding unless it uncovers stronger causal evaluations separating Biden-era federal action from inflation, congressional expiration decisions, and state implementation.

## Artifact Y

## 1. Orientation

### Authority and practical control

Joe Biden controlled the federal executive branch in 2022: presidential directives, agency leadership, regulatory priorities, grant administration, enforcement, and implementation of enacted appropriations. He could propose legislation and veto or sign bills, but Congress controlled enactment and appropriations. Courts constrained executive action. States, territories, tribes, school districts, public-health departments, and local housing agencies delivered much of the welfare portfolio.

Attribution therefore varies:

- **Direct presidential:** executive orders, bill signatures, and explicit administration priorities.
- **Shared federal:** statutes such as the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA), PACT Act, American Rescue Plan (ARP), and Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act required Congress as well as presidential advocacy and signature.
- **Intergovernmental:** Medicaid, vaccination, schools, rental assistance, water systems, and homelessness services depended heavily on state/local implementation.
- **Limited outcome attribution:** poverty, mortality, food insecurity, learning, inflation, and insurance coverage also reflected inherited institutions, private behavior, pandemic dynamics, Federal Reserve policy, and global shocks.

### Inherited baseline

Biden inherited a wealthy, high-capacity but unusually unequal welfare system: nearly universal basic water and sanitation, mass public education, Medicare and Social Security, yet no universal health coverage, high medical cost exposure, substantial racial and geographic disparities, housing shortages, and fragmented federal-state administration.

The immediate baseline was distorted by COVID-19. Temporary 2020–2021 relief—including stimulus payments, enhanced unemployment support, the expanded Child Tax Credit, enhanced ACA subsidies, continuous Medicaid enrollment, rental assistance, and nutrition waivers—had produced unusually low measured supplemental poverty in 2021. Their expiration makes a simple 2021–2022 comparison misleading.

Important current measurement warning: in July 2026 BLS disclosed coding errors in Supplemental Poverty Measure thresholds for 2019–2024. Census has warned that revised poverty rates are forthcoming. The originally published 2022 SPM estimates below remain useful provisionally, but researchers should replace them when corrected estimates appear.

### Shocks and constraints

The 2022 record was shaped by:

- continuing COVID-19, including the early-2022 Omicron wave;
- inflation following pandemic disruption, fiscal support, supply constraints, and energy/food shocks;
- Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and related commodity-price pressure;
- pandemic learning loss and school-workforce disruption;
- expiration of congressionally authorized emergency benefits;
- federalism and wide state variation;
- the Supreme Court’s *Dobbs* decision, which sharply reduced reproductive-health access in many states;
- congressional refusal to renew the expanded Child Tax Credit;
- judicial stays against broad student-debt cancellation.

### Information environment

This is an open, heavily scrutinized case. Census, CDC/NCHS, NCES, USDA, CMS, Treasury, HUD, VA, GAO, courts, household surveys, and independent research provide substantial evidence. The main risks are not censorship but:

- confusing announcements with delivery;
- counting one federal-state program as wholly presidential;
- treating the exceptional 2021 relief baseline as normal;
- conflating 2022 enactment with benefits beginning in later years;
- relying on administrative enrollment rather than effective access or quality;
- double-counting official releases that draw on the same dataset.

No evidence found suggests that benefits were systematically conditioned on loyalty to Biden. Absence of detected national politicization is not proof that every local administrator acted neutrally.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact name | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-6B-001 | 2022 poverty reversal | Expired relief materially worsened child and overall disposable-resource poverty. |
| WEB-6B-002 | Insurance coverage improved | Shows a delivered access gain, though not affordable care by itself. |
| WEB-6B-003 | Marketplace enrollment record | Connects ARP subsidies and federal outreach to measurable uptake. |
| WEB-6B-004 | IRA health institutions | Durable 2022 legislation addressing premiums and drug costs, mostly prospectively. |
| WEB-6B-005 | Medical affordability remained poor | Insurance gains coexisted with widespread delayed care and financial insecurity. |
| WEB-6B-006 | Rental assistance delivery and oversight | Large vulnerable-group delivery, with incomplete data and uneven administration. |
| WEB-6B-007 | Food insecurity increased | Important contrary outcome after emergency supports receded amid inflation. |
| WEB-6B-008 | Severe learning loss | Documents inherited/persisting educational harm and unequal recovery needs. |
| WEB-6B-009 | Evidence-oriented school recovery funds | Shows targeted federal recovery design, but limited national outcome proof in 2022. |
| WEB-6B-010 | Water infrastructure implementation | Long-horizon investment aimed at lead, contaminants, and disadvantaged communities. |
| WEB-6B-011 | Mortality and COVID-19 improvement | Major crisis outcome improved, but causal attribution is shared and incomplete. |
| WEB-6B-012 | Federal vaccine-equity programs | Favorable targeted implementation with independently audited residual disparities. |
| WEB-6B-013 | Young-child vaccination inequity | Strong contrary evidence on uptake, geography, race, and implementation limits. |
| WEB-6B-014 | PACT Act delivery began | Durable expansion for toxic-exposed veterans with measurable 2022 implementation. |
| WEB-6B-015 | PSLF correction delivered relief | Administrative correction produced actual debt forgiveness for public servants. |
| WEB-6B-016 | Broad student-debt plan failed | Welfare-oriented announcement lacked delivered benefit and exceeded statutory authority. |
| WEB-6B-017 | Reproductive-access response | Federal action protected some dignity/access after *Dobbs*, but state disparities remained. |
| WEB-6B-018 | Homelessness persisted | Shows housing need remained large despite emergency eviction prevention. |

## 3. Ten-lens disposition

1. **6B.1 — Welfare as a core purpose:** Well evidenced but mixed. The IRA, PACT Act, ARP implementation, ACA outreach, water funding, rental assistance, and reproductive-health orders reveal sustained welfare attention (WEB-6B-003, 004, 006, 009, 010, 014, 017). Expiration of the enhanced CTC and other relief, however, produced a sharp poverty reversal (WEB-6B-001).

2. **6B.2 — Basic-service access:** Health insurance and Marketplace enrollment improved, rental assistance reached millions, and water and education recovery investments entered implementation (WEB-6B-002, 003, 006, 009, 010). Effective affordability remained poor; food insecurity increased; learning loss and homelessness remained substantial (WEB-6B-005, 007, 008, 018).

3. **6B.3 — Vulnerable groups:** Strong design and partial-delivery evidence for low-income renters, racial minorities, disadvantaged water systems, students furthest from opportunity, veterans, older people, and women (WEB-6B-004, 006, 009, 010, 012, 014, 017). Child poverty and vaccination disparities are major contrary evidence (WEB-6B-001, 013).

4. **6B.4 — Professional administration:** Federal programs were managed through established agencies and audited by GAO rather than personal patronage networks. Nevertheless, ERA data were incomplete, allocation did not consistently follow need, and local capacity varied (WEB-6B-006). Student-debt and loan-servicing administration also displayed legal, tracking, and fraud-control weaknesses (WEB-6B-015, 016).

5. **6B.5 — Evidence and correction:** Strong evidence exists for standardized measurement, targeted-site selection, evidence-based school-recovery requirements, demographic monitoring, GAO oversight, and ERA reallocations (WEB-6B-006, 009, 012, 013). Correction was incomplete: Treasury lacked recipient/payment data, educational outcomes remained poor, and vaccine disparities persisted.

6. **6B.6 — Crisis suffering:** COVID-associated mortality declined sharply and emergency rental assistance likely prevented evictions (WEB-6B-006, 011). The decline cannot be credited wholly to Biden: prior infection, vaccination developed and initiated under the previous administration, treatment advances, state decisions, and viral evolution all mattered. Food insecurity and child poverty worsened as temporary supports expired (WEB-6B-001, 007).

7. **6B.7 — Political conditionality:** No credible evidence found of Biden conditioning ordinary federal welfare benefits on personal or partisan loyalty. Formula allocations, statutory eligibility, professional agencies, courts, and audits constrained such conduct. This lens is supported mainly by institutional structure and the absence of substantiated national allegations, so confidence is moderate rather than conclusive.

8. **6B.8 — Dignity and equal opportunity:** The record includes affirmative attention to racial vaccine equity, low-income renters, toxic-exposed veterans, reproductive care, and disadvantaged communities (WEB-6B-006, 010, 012, 014, 017). Persistent racial vaccination gaps, cost barriers, child poverty, learning inequality, and state-dependent reproductive access limit the result (WEB-6B-001, 005, 008, 013, 017).

9. **6B.9 — Institutional durability:** The IRA’s drug-pricing framework, PACT Act entitlements, and multi-year water funding are the clearest durable measures (WEB-6B-004, 010, 014). ERA and ARP education aid were temporary. Broad student cancellation was legally invalidated. Whether local eviction-prevention and learning-recovery capacity survived the grants requires later evaluation.

10. **6B.10 — Life chances:** The 2022 trajectory was genuinely mixed. Coverage expanded, COVID mortality declined, renters received protection, public servants obtained debt relief, veterans gained durable entitlements, and future health/water institutions were strengthened. Simultaneously, real household income fell, child poverty and food insecurity rose, learning loss was severe, medical affordability remained weak, and reproductive access became more unequal. Attribution is strongest for enacted institutional changes and administrative implementation, weaker for annual national outcomes.

### Domain coverage

- **Strong:** health coverage, health affordability, pandemic response, housing assistance, poverty/social protection, food security, education outcomes, vulnerable-group distribution.
- **Moderate:** water infrastructure, veterans’ protection, reproductive dignity, administrative quality.
- **Weak or unavailable:** sanitation-specific change, childcare quality, disability services outside veterans and vaccination access, long-run housing supply delivery, political conditionality at subnational level, and 2022 causal evaluations of education recovery.

No chapter score is assigned.

## 4. Source-state lists

### Opened corroboration

- Census 2022 income/poverty/insurance press release corroborates WEB-6B-001 and 002, but depends on the same CPS ASEC reports.
- Treasury’s March 2022 ERA release corroborates payment volume; it is not independent of Treasury administrative data.
- Treasury’s demographic ERA release corroborates targeting but uses the same grantee reporting later audited by GAO.
- VA’s September and December 2022 releases corroborate PACT uptake; both depend on VA administrative records.
- CDC’s adult vaccination survey corroborates broader access disparities but measures all recommended adult vaccines rather than the federal COVID programs specifically.
- Commonwealth Fund provides independent survey corroboration for affordability limitations.
- Supreme Court’s 2023 opinion independently establishes the legal failure of the 2022 debt plan.

### Reused supplied sources

All five supplied sources were opened before use:

- E014 → WEB-6B-012.
- E015 → opened and retained as corroboration, not a separate central record, because its composite includes several non-COVID vaccines.
- E016 → WEB-6B-013.
- E017 → WEB-6B-005.
- E018 → opened; retained as official implementation corroboration, not a separate accepted fact because it substantially overlaps WEB-6B-012.

### Uninspected or partially inspected leads

- UNDP country tables and V-Dem equality priors: useful for structured baseline comparison but unlikely to discriminate Biden’s 2022 contribution.
- Corrected 2019–2024 SPM estimates: not yet published by Census as of July 24, 2026.
- Full state-by-state ARP ESSER expenditure and evaluation files.
- EPA state-level 2022 obligation/disbursement files and verified completed lead-service-line replacements.
- HUD’s detailed 2022 AHAR subgroup tables.
- State abortion-access, maternal-health, and interstate-travel datasets following *Dobbs*.
- Disability-specific SSI, HCBS, and special-education implementation data.
- 2022 SNAP participation, benefit adequacy, and emergency-allotment variation by state.

### Rejected

- Pure White House promotional summaries without underlying administrative, statutory, or evaluation evidence.
- 2023–2026 IRA benefit counts presented as if they were realized in 2022.
- The 2021 record-low poverty rate as a 2022 outcome.
- Broad student-debt cancellation as delivered welfare: no debt was discharged under that program.
- Treasury claims that ERA alone caused below-baseline eviction filings: accepted only in the more cautious GAO form because moratoria and local conditions also mattered.
- Education pages dominated by philanthropic pledges rather than federal delivery.
- Adult vaccination composite coverage as a direct measure of Biden’s COVID response.

### Access-blocked

- Direct Congress.gov pages were initially blocked by automated access controls. CRS and authenticated public-law pages indexed from Congress.gov were accessible and used instead.
- Local researcher-guide and source-registry files could not be inspected because the workspace shell was unavailable in this session.
- No paywalled news source was necessary for a central claim.

### Counts

Counting unique substantive source pages/documents rather than search-result duplicates:

- **Discovered:** 48
- **Opened or substantively extracted:** 31
- **Accepted as developed evidence:** 18
- **Reused from supplied list:** 5 opened; 3 became central records and 2 corroboration
- **Opened corroboration not separately accepted:** 7
- **Rejected:** 8 source/claim uses
- **Blocked:** 2 access categories
- **Uninspected or only partially inspected leads:** 8

## 5. Remaining research and saturation

Additional broad national searching is now mostly repeating the same 2022 statutes, administrative totals, and outcome datasets. The largest potential gains would come from narrower work:

- corrected SPM estimates;
- independent causal evaluations of ARP ESSER learning recovery;
- state-level water project completion rather than allocation;
- reproductive and maternal-health outcomes after *Dobbs*;
- disability and long-term-care services;
- subnational ERA and welfare-administration disparities.

Those inquiries could materially refine distribution, implementation quality, and durability, but are unlikely to reverse the basic finding that 2022 combined meaningful institutional gains with worsening poverty, food security, education, and affordability indicators.

- Remaining discovery leads not retained: 5
- Distinct accepted source organizations: 8
- Source types: official electoral rulings, official court/docket records, official audit, wire-service reporting, fact-checking investigation, comparative civil-society monitoring, press-association report, and investigative press reporting

Lens coverage:

- `7B.1`: Strong evidence of repeated unsupported election claims, the ambassadors meeting, and the food-insecurity denial.
- `7B.2`: Evidence of repetition/doubling down and treatment of journalists; no well-documented 2022 example of Bolsonaro voluntarily correcting a material false claim.
- `7B.3`: Stronger for mixing incumbency resources and reelection activity; weak for business, licensing, procurement, or foreign-policy conflicts.
- `7B.4`: Residual major gap. Family-property reporting is allegation/context only. No target-period adjudication establishes personal or close-family profit from office.
- `7B.5`: Residual major gap. Military and loyalist appointments are documented in the source landscape, but the wave found insufficient appointment-by-appointment evidence connecting 2022 selections to incompetence, self-dealing, or yes-man protection.
- `7B.6`: Mixed evidence: multiple investigations, audits, courts, media, and fact-checkers operated; secrecy and attacks created friction, but complete intolerance of investigation is not established.
- `7B.7`: Evidence includes use of state communications/resources, the Silveira pardon, and pressure on accountability institutions; the Federal Police no-crime finding is material contrary evidence.
- `7B.8`: Repeated election narratives and disputed campaign-period policy shifts are relevant, but systematic promise-keeping evidence remains limited.
- `7B.9`: Evidence supports favoritism/transactional-politics concerns in pardons and campaign-linked public-resource use. Procurement favoritism and personal enforcement favors remain unproven.
- `7B.10`: Strong pattern evidence concerning misinformation, institutional attacks, press treatment, and public/private-role boundaries, tempered by functioning judicial, audit, media, and electoral checks.

Another Chapter 7B-specific search wave would have only targeted value, chiefly for `7B.4` and `7B.5`: primary asset declarations, procurement records, appointment files, and final judicial records could materially improve those lenses. A broad repeat search is unlikely to add much because public-claim, election-resource, pardon, press-treatment, and audit evidence is already saturated and carries high duplicate-event risk.

## Artifact Y

## 1. Orientation

### Authority and practical control

Jair Bolsonaro was president throughout 2022, the final year of his 2019–2022 term. He formally controlled the federal executive, presidential communications and premises, ministerial appointments, senior appointments in the Federal Police, Federal Highway Police and intelligence apparatus, execution of the federal budget, and the constitutional pardon power. He did not control Congress, the courts, the TSE, the TCU, state governments, independent prosecutors, or all police forces.

In practice, coalition dependence—especially on the Centrão—constrained appointments and spending. Military officers, political allies and personally trusted aides nevertheless occupied unusually prominent federal posts. These circumstances support attribution where Bolsonaro personally spoke, issued a decree, used presidential resources, selected or protected an official, or was adjudicated to have led relevant conduct; they do not make every ministerial or coalition scandal his own.

### Inherited baseline

Brazil entered 2022 with:

- A functioning but contested anti-corruption and accountability framework: CGU, TCU, Federal Police, prosecutors, courts, the Access to Information Law, election auditing, and active independent media.
- Long-standing clientelism and corruption risks predating Bolsonaro.
- The Car Wash task force terminated in 2021 amid both legitimate due-process criticism and concern that anti-corruption enforcement was weakening.
- Existing allegations involving Bolsonaro relatives and associates, but no established 2022 finding that Bolsonaro personally obtained proceeds from the older “rachadinha” or property matters. BTI expressly said that, as of its review period, Bolsonaro himself had not been implicated in a corruption scandal, while relatives and associates were suspected. [BTI 2022, pp. 11 and 34](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2022_BRA.pdf)

### Shocks and constraints

The election, post-pandemic recovery, inflation, food insecurity and Russia’s invasion of Ukraine shaped the year. Congress and the courts constrained executive action. These shocks may explain policy reversals or fiscal tradeoffs; they do not explain unsupported electoral claims, conversion of official events into campaign assets, concealment of family-access records, or a later-adjudicated attempt to remain in power.

### Information environment

Criticism and investigation remained possible. The TSE, STF, TCU, CGU, prosecutors, police, journalists and fact-checkers produced extensive records. This creates greater visibility than in a closed system, so article volume alone should not be treated as severity. The strongest evidence here consists of ruler statements, contemporaneous orders, judicial findings and the later final criminal judgment—not complaint counts.

A methodological limitation: the workspace was read-only and exposed no filesystem-reading command, so the local guide and registry could not be inspected directly. The supplied local-first findings were used as the starting corpus, and each accepted web source was opened or its underlying official search extract inspected.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact name | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-7B-001 | Ambassador meeting and electoral falsehoods | TSE found personal misuse of office and state media to spread unsupported claims for reelection. |
| WEB-7B-002 | Final coup-plot conviction | Later final judgment attributes an organized effort to remain in power directly to Bolsonaro and senior appointees. |
| WEB-7B-003 | Campaign livestreams from presidential premises | Contemporaneous TSE order found indications that exclusive public assets funded campaign content. |
| WEB-7B-004 | Bicentennial events captured for campaign | TSE adjudicated deliberate confusion between the presidency and Bolsonaro’s private electoral interest. |
| WEB-7B-005 | Recurrent false-statement corpus | Documents repetition across election, corruption and other subjects, with no general correction pattern. |
| WEB-7B-006 | Hunger denial | Direct campaign-period statement contradicted contemporaneous FAO estimates. |
| WEB-7B-007 | Daniel Silveira pardon | STF found Bolsonaro’s individual pardon of a political ally constitutionally invalid because of improper purpose. |
| WEB-7B-008 | Family and ally access secrecy | Records show official withholding of visitor information, with a security/privacy defense that limits the inference. |
| WEB-7B-009 | “Secret budget” | STF found the RP-9 arrangement unconstitutional; executive personal responsibility is shared and incompletely established. |
| WEB-7B-010 | MEC pastors and alleged investigation leak | Raises favoritism and obstruction concerns but remained allegation-level as to Bolsonaro. |
| WEB-7B-011 | Saudi gifts/jewelry | Serious retrospective personal-benefit inquiry, but no final merits finding; PGR later sought archiving on legal-clarity grounds. |
| WEB-7B-012 | Family cash-property reporting | Documentary reporting raised unexplained-asset questions but did not prove illicit origin or office-derived benefit. |
| WEB-7B-013 | 2022 federal accounts approved with reservations | Important contrary evidence against treating the entire administration as unaudited or its accounts as rejected. |
| WEB-7B-014 | Transition administratively authorized | Limited favorable conduct, later outweighed as evidence of democratic commitment by the coup judgment. |
| WEB-7B-015 | Independent inquiries operated | Covaxin was archived and several other inquiries proceeded, showing real checks and some exculpatory outcomes. |
| WEB-7B-016 | PRF director’s partisan use of office | Later judicial finding against Bolsonaro’s appointee, but personal direction or notice by Bolsonaro was not independently proved. |

## 3. Disposition of the ten evidence lenses

The authoritative lens wording is reproduced unchanged.

| ID | Evidence lens | Disposition |
|---|---|---|
| **7B.1** | Does the ruler habitually tell the truth to the public, legislature, courts, allies, and international partners, especially on matters where deception would protect power or reputation? | Substantial adverse evidence. Bolsonaro personally repeated unsupported election claims to the public and foreign ambassadors, denied measured food insecurity, and maintained recurrent false or distorted narratives. The strongest material deception protected reelection and later retention of power. WEB-7B-001, 002, 005, 006. |
| **7B.2** | Does the ruler admit errors, correct false claims, and allow truthful reporting, or do they knowingly mislead, double down, blame others, and punish truth-tellers? | Adverse pattern of repetition rather than correction. The record contains no comparable pattern of voluntary corrections. He blamed media and electoral institutions even after institutional rebuttals. Criticism and checking nevertheless remained possible. WEB-7B-001, 005, 006, 015. |
| **7B.3** | Does the ruler separate personal/family/business interests from state decisions, public contracts, licensing, regulation, law enforcement, and foreign policy? | Material failures in separating office from campaign interest are adjudicated. Family-access secrecy and the gifts inquiry raise additional concerns but require caution. No strong evidence was found of a Bolsonaro-owned business receiving a 2022 contract or regulatory favor. WEB-7B-003, 004, 008, 011. |
| **7B.4** | Does the ruler or close family profit from office through assets, contracts, monopolies, gifts, bribes, emoluments, insider access, opaque foundations, or hidden conflicts of interest? | Serious but partly unresolved evidence. The jewelry inquiry concerned potentially privatized official gifts, but the PGR’s 2026 archiving request and absence of a final merits judgment prevent treating criminal extraction as proved. Property reporting is an asset-source warning, not proof of profit from the presidency. WEB-7B-011, 012. |
| **7B.5** | Does the ruler appoint competent professionals, or fill government with family, friends, cronies, donors, business associates, loyalists, and yes-men to protect personal power or self-dealing? | Evidence supports loyalty-heavy appointments and a senior appointee’s partisan conduct, while the coup judgment identifies several first-echelon officials in the core organization. Evidence is thinner on systematic competence comparisons or direct self-dealing through appointments. WEB-7B-002, 010, 016. |
| **7B.6** | Does the ruler tolerate independent investigation of their conduct, assets, campaign finance, conflicts of interest, associates, and concealed official decisions? | Mixed but substantially adverse. Numerous inquiries, audits and court cases operated, and some ended favorably to Bolsonaro. Against that, family-access opacity, attempted secrecy and alleged warning of an investigated minister show resistance. The final coup proceeding itself received evidence and defense submissions. WEB-7B-002, 008, 010, 013, 015. |
| **7B.7** | Does the ruler use state power to conceal illegal, destructive, or self-serving activity, protect themselves from accountability, punish investigators, or neutralize courts, prosecutors, auditors, media, and whistleblowers? | Strong adverse evidence where the personal nexus is clearest: official communications and events were used for reelection; an ally received a purpose-invalid pardon; and the later final judgment found an organized attempt to stay in power. The MEC leak remains disputed and carries less weight. WEB-7B-001–004, 007, 010. |
| **7B.8** | Does the ruler keep promises and respect formal commitments, or opportunistically reverse positions, manipulate public information, and conceal tradeoffs for personal advantage? | Adverse evidence centers on his constitutional and anti-corruption commitments versus deliberate electoral misinformation and the later-adjudicated attempt to overturn succession. Authorizing administrative transition is limited contrary evidence. WEB-7B-001, 002, 005, 014. |
| **7B.9** | Does the ruler avoid nepotism, favoritism, clientelism, and transactional politics in appointments, pardons, procurement, enforcement, and access to public information? | Favoritism is clearest in the Silveira pardon and capture of public events for allied campaigns. RP-9 and MEC evidence indicate clientelism risks but have shared or disputed attribution. No sufficiently developed 2022 procurement record establishes personal favoritism by Bolsonaro. WEB-7B-004, 007, 009, 010, 016. |
| **7B.10** | Does the ruler model ethical standards that improve public trust, or normalize deliberate lying, impunity, self-dealing, conflicts of interest, and cynicism? | Convergent adverse evidence: repeated false claims, deliberate office/campaign conflation, favoritism toward an ally, and ultimately a final conviction for attempting to retain power. Functioning audits and investigations mitigate claims of total impunity but do not supply affirmative costly candor. WEB-7B-001–007, 013, 015. |

## 4. Source-state lists

### Opened independent corroboration

- AP reporting on the Federal Police’s 884-page coup investigation and military-command testimony; later superseded for the core claim by the final STF conviction.
- Reuters reporting on Bolsonaro’s transition statement, hunger statement, MEC investigation and property response.
- MPF account of the later TRF2 judgment against former PRF director Silvinei Vasques.
- TCU records concerning the 2022 accounts and Saudi gifts.
- Aos Fatos’ statement corpus, independently checked against TSE adjudication for the most material electoral claims.
- BTI 2022 for inherited institutions, press environment, and the important contrary observation that Bolsonaro was not then personally accused of corruption.

### Reused supplied sources

- E040: presidential-palace campaign livestreams.
- E045: denial of the food-insecurity estimate.
- E049: BTI integrity and corruption baseline.
- E050: family-access and transparency allegations, used only where official CGU material supplied support or a limiting defense.
- E029: dismissal of the Flávio Bolsonaro case, retained as contrary context rather than proof about Jair.
- E030: unsupported electoral-fraud claims, strengthened by the TSE merits ruling.
- E035: military presence, used only as appointment context.
- E051: later coup allegation, superseded by the 2025 final STF conviction.

### Promising sources still needing inspection

- Full AP 2668 judgment and individual votes, especially the dissent, for a more granular chronology of every 2022 overt act.
- Full TSE judgments in AIJE 0600814-85 and the Bicentennial cases rather than the court’s detailed official summaries.
- Complete 2026 PGR jewelry opinion and any post-March 2026 STF disposition.
- Full INQ 4831 Federal Police report and final prosecutorial disposition on alleged interference in the Federal Police.
- Bolsonaro/Braga Netto 2022 campaign-account judgment; search results did not expose a reliable final record.
- Appointment-level dataset connecting individual federal posts to family, donor or business ties.
- Final merits record, if any, for the MEC pastors investigation and alleged advance warning.
- Audited asset declarations reconciled to the UOL property records.

### Rejected or given zero decisive weight

- General CPI, WGI or V-Dem corruption scores: no personal nexus.
- General press-attack, Indigenous, environmental, policing and COVID conditions without a specific Bolsonaro integrity nexus.
- Later vaccination-record allegations as decisive evidence: the PGR sought termination, and available material did not establish Bolsonaro’s personal direction to the required standard.
- Flávio Bolsonaro’s “rachadinha” allegation as evidence against Jair: distinct person, annulled evidence and no proved personal benefit.
- Property purchases as corruption proof: cash payment is legal and the source of funds was not established.
- RP-9 as solely Bolsonaro’s scheme: Congress had substantial design and allocation authority.
- Article-count or fact-check-count arithmetic: retained only as evidence of repetition and domains, not as a score proxy.
- Unadjudicated jewelry allegations as proof of theft, bribery or foreign-policy exchange.

### Access-blocked

- Several STF news pages returned 403 or internal fetch errors; their official search extracts and process pages were available.
- The CGU 2022 transparency page redirected to a login page on opening; the indexed official response remained available.
- The direct TCU constitutional-jurisprudence page and some MPF pages returned internal/cache errors.
- The March 2026 Agência Brasil jewelry update timed out on opening.
- Some Reuters URLs were accessible only through licensed UOL syndication.
- Local workspace guides and registries could not be read because no filesystem-reading tool was exposed.

## 5. Counts

Counts are source-level, not search-result snippets:

- Distinct potentially relevant sources discovered: **55**
- Underlying sources or detailed official records opened/inspected: **27**
- Sources accepted into developed evidence records: **22**
- Developed evidence records: **16**
- Supplied sources materially reused: **8**
- Sources or proposed uses rejected/zero-weighted: **9**
- Access-blocked sources: **6**

A source may be both reused and accepted; therefore categories do not sum arithmetically.

## 6. Remaining questions and saturation

The research is saturated on public deception, electoral self-interest, misuse of public campaign resources, the Silveira pardon and the 2022 power-retention effort. Further general searching is unlikely to alter those conclusions because they rest on adjudicated findings.

More research could materially improve:

- Whether the jewelry matter ultimately produced a final post-March 2026 disposition.
- Whether Bolsonaro personally directed or knowingly protected MEC, PRF or police-interference misconduct.
- Systematic appointment competence and donor/family ties.
- Personal beneficial ownership, unexplained wealth and campaign-finance reconciliation.
- Affirmative examples of voluntary correction, costly candor or acceptance of scrutiny; none were located, but absence is not proof.

No score is assigned.

# Sheikh Hasina — 4B (Bangladesh)


## Artifact X

Chapter 4B research is complete for Sheikh Hasina, Bangladesh, 2022 only. No scoring was performed.

## Findings by lens

- **4B.1–4B.2 — Contestability and entrenchment:** Bangladesh entered 2022 with the disputed 2018 election still defining the electoral environment. In January 2022, Parliament finally enacted the constitutionally contemplated Election Commission appointment law. This was a potentially favorable institutional development, but it passed rapidly under the Awami League’s overwhelming majority; opposition requests for public review were defeated, and the president’s appointments remained structurally linked to prime-ministerial advice. Hasina argued that opposition amendments had been accepted. The evidentiary balance therefore supports formal procedural improvement accompanied by substantial independence concerns. [The Daily Star](https://www.thedailystar.net/news/bangladesh/news/ec-bill-passed-snubbing-all-concerns-2948716), [Prothom Alo](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/government/gazette-of-ec-formation-bill-published-president-to-form-search-committee), [2022 US human-rights report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2089131.html).

- **4B.3 — Tolerance of opposition, protest, journalism and monitoring:** From August through December, police, Awami League supporters and BNP protesters were involved in repeated violent confrontations. Reports documented opposition deaths and injuries, mass arrests, restrictions surrounding rallies, and attacks by ruling-party affiliates. The NGO Affairs Bureau also denied Odhikar’s registration renewal because its reporting allegedly damaged the state’s image. Government and allied-party voices disputed opposition narratives, while some Awami League coalition leaders publicly condemned attacks. [HRW](https://www.hrw.org/news/2022/10/10/bangladesh-crackdown-political-opposition), [Amnesty](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2022/12/bangladesh-authorities-must-end-repression-of-demonstrations/), [HRW/Odhikar](https://www.hrw.org/news/2022/06/09/bangladesh-stop-punishing-human-rights-work).

- **4B.4 — Independent institutions:** BTI described prime-ministerial concentration, Article 70 party discipline, missing parliamentary oversight, executive influence over courts, and politicized judicial appointments. The EC law created a formal search committee—including senior judicial and audit officials—but did not clearly insulate appointments from executive influence. These conditions were substantially inherited rather than newly created in 2022. [BTI 2022, pp. 9–10](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2022_BGD.pdf).

- **4B.5 — Party capture and politicization:** The US report recorded use of law-enforcement resources for charges against opposition leaders and violence or intimidation by Awami League-affiliated organizations with reported impunity. BTI linked control of both government and ruling party in Hasina’s hands to weak legislative checks. Direct 2022 evidence of arbitrary personal loyalty tests or a newly expanded personality cult was not found. [US report, Section 3](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2089131.html), [BTI 2022](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2022_BGD.pdf).

- **4B.6 — Media and information:** Authorities continued DSA arrests and prosecutions. One 2022 case involved a journalist reporting alleged irregularities connected to a local Awami League figure. OHCHR found DSA provisions vague, excessively broad and capable of chilling public-interest debate; the government did not suspend the law. A limiting fact is that the law minister acknowledged misuse and reportedly advised against immediate arrest in less-serious cases, but this did not amount to repeal or demonstrated systemic reform. [CPJ](https://cpj.org/2022/06/bangladesh-journalist-arrested-2-charged-under-digital-security-act/), [OHCHR technical note, pp. 1–7](https://www.ohchr.org/sites/default/files/documents/countries/bangladesh/OHCHR-Technical-Note-on-review-of-the-Digital-Security-Act-June-2022.pdf), [Freedom on the Net 2022](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-net/2022).

- **4B.7 — Political equality:** No law formally barred women or minorities from political participation, and 50 parliamentary seats were reserved for women. One required search-committee member under the new EC law was also female. Nevertheless, parties missed their 33-percent committee-membership target, women in reserved seats were selected indirectly, and religious, ethnic, Indigenous and LGBTQI+ groups remained underrepresented. [US report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2089131.html), [The Daily Star](https://www.thedailystar.net/news/bangladesh/news/ec-bill-passed-snubbing-all-concerns-2948716).

- **4B.8 — Succession and transfer:** No constitutional prime-ministerial term limit applied. No national transfer or succession event occurred in 2022, so actual compliance with transfer rules cannot be tested in-period. Hasina publicly claimed Bangladesh could conduct elections and peaceful transfers, but inherited election deficiencies and late-2022 opposition repression weakened that assurance. This lens remains partly unobservable for 2022.

- **4B.9 — Digital controls and administrative harassment:** Later reporting tied multiple 2022 mobile-internet disruptions to BNP rallies in Khulna, Barishal, Faridpur, Sylhet, Cumilla, Rajshahi and Dhaka. The government/BTRC reportedly denied ordering shutdowns, so attribution remains disputed. Separately, the proposed Data Protection Act would have allowed remote access to encrypted communications, broad government directions and liability exemptions, but it was only a draft in 2022. [Freedom on the Net 2023, lines documenting 2022 events](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-net/2023), [Amnesty](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2022/04/bangladesh-new-data-protection-bill-threatens-peoples-right-to-privacy/).

- **4B.10 — Inherited-to-left trajectory:** The strongest defensible inference is that democratic resilience remained weak and experienced additional adverse pressure during late 2022. The EC appointment statute was a limited formal improvement; it was outweighed in practical effect by opposition repression, continued DSA enforcement, Odhikar’s deregistration and rally-associated connectivity restrictions. A precise year-on-year causal estimate remains unavailable because several institutional defects predated 2022 and the shutdown orders were disputed.

## Reused, independently verified evidence

Seven prior records were reopened and verified for this chapter: E001, E006, E007, E008, E009, E011 and E059. They are not counted as newly discovered documents. E038’s shutdown proposition was independently checked through Freedom House’s underlying 2022 event chronology and retained as new unit WEB-4B-009 with explicit attribution uncertainty.

- Plausible documents discovered or screened: **41**

- Source types: official/legal records, UN legal analysis, comparative assessments, foreign-government annual reporting, international NGO event reports, press-freedom case documentation, democracy-event tracking and domestic legislative journalism.

All ten lenses received a dedicated search. The exact residual gaps are:

- **4B.5:** no strong ruler-specific 2022 evidence of personal loyalty tests or a newly intensified personality cult.
- **4B.8:** no succession or transfer event occurred; absence of prime-ministerial term limits is observable, compliance with an actual transfer is not.
- **4B.9:** shutdown timing is well reported, but documentary proof of government/BTRC orders remains disputed.
- **4B.10:** late-2022 deterioration is supportable, but a clean one-year causal trajectory separating Hasina’s 2022 decisions from inherited post-2018 conditions is not.

A further general Chapter 4B search wave is unlikely to add much. A narrowly targeted archival request for BTRC shutdown directives, cabinet papers on the Data Protection Bill, or EC search-committee nomination records could materially reduce the specific gaps above.

The workspace was exposed as read-only and no filesystem read/write execution facility was available, so `research-ledger-manifest.json` could not be inspected or updated safely. A genuine cumulative replacement cannot be reconstructed from the lead index alone without risking deletion of prior unseen entries; the ten `SOURCE_CLAIM_JSON` records above are the deterministic append payload for the parent-owned cumulative manifest.

## Artifact Y

## Scope and research status

This dossier assesses Sheikh Hasina’s record during calendar year 2022 only. Evidence inherited from earlier years is marked accordingly; later publications are used only when they report or analyze 2022 events.

No chapter score is assigned. The evidence strongly establishes an authoritarian and erosive pattern, but scoring and calibration against the smoke-test batch remain tasks for the chapter judge.

A technical limitation affected the prescribed local-first procedure: the workspace shell and local registry were unavailable. I therefore used the supplied local-prior description and source-confidence cues, attempted to open every underlying web source, and did not treat search-result text alone as fully extracted evidence.

## 1. Orientation

### Authority

Bangladesh formally had a parliamentary system, but practical executive authority was highly concentrated. The prime minister headed both the government and the dominant Awami League. Article 70’s anti-defection rule allowed party leadership to prevent governing-party MPs from voting independently, while the AL’s overwhelming parliamentary majority left little effective legislative opposition. BTI described this combination as making separation of powers largely illusory and leaving the bureaucracy, Election Commission, judiciary, and parliamentary oversight vulnerable to executive or partisan control (WEB-4B-001, WEB-4B-002).

Attribution should nevertheless be differentiated:

- Hasina was directly attributable for public statements, cabinet direction, governing-party leadership, and her decision to award medals to sanctioned RAB commanders.
- Executive institutions—including police, BTRC, the NGO Affairs Bureau, ministries, and the Prime Minister’s Office—support authority-based or institutional attribution.
- Violence by AL or Chhatra League activists is shared attribution unless direct orders are established. The stronger attributable issue is reported impunity and police failure to protect opposition events.
- Courts, the president, Election Commission, and local officials retained formal powers, but evidence indicates weak de facto independence.

### Inherited baseline

Hasina entered 2022 already governing from a severely compromised electoral baseline:

- The 2018 parliamentary election had been marked by intimidation, arrests, violence, fraud allegations, restrictions on observers, and an AL-led result of 288 out of 300 directly elected seats.
- The BNP had been organizationally weakened; Khaleda Zia remained under a suspended sentence and travel restrictions.
- BTI’s review described the legislature as lacking meaningful checks, courts as politicized, and the bureaucracy and Election Commission as partisan.
- The Digital Security Act of 2018 already supplied broad powers over online expression, arrest, search, blocking, and content removal.

These inherited conditions are not newly charged to 2022, but Hasina’s government retained and used them rather than materially remedying them.

### Shocks and constraints

COVID recovery, inflation, fuel-price increases, Ukraine-war pressures, floods and climate shocks, Rohingya displacement, and Myanmar spillover constrained government capacity and helped prompt opposition demonstrations. They do not explain the selective arrests, rally-related connectivity restrictions, refusal to reform the DSA, or harassment of monitors. Bangladesh was not under a declared constitutional emergency.

Political violence was not one-sided. The BNP and AL had a long history of confrontational politics; both sides accused the other of instigating 2022 clashes. That limits attribution for individual street incidents but not for police conduct, selective arrest patterns, regulatory restrictions, or failures to investigate ruling-party violence.

### Information environment

Domestic reporting continued, and international organizations, foreign governments, NGOs, and some local outlets documented abuses. This shows that criticism was possible, not eliminated. But self-censorship, blocked outlets, DSA exposure, advertising pressure, surveillance, attacks, NGO-registration controls, and possible retaliation against relatives create substantial underreporting risk. Repeated NGO, diplomatic, and press accounts often trace back to the same local reports or incidents and should not be counted as independent events.
