## Lens coverage and residual gaps

- **3B.1:** Strong adverse coverage: enforced disappearance, torture, political imprisonment, custodial death, and alleged extrajudicial killing. Gap: no complete nationwide denominator or verified presidential command chain.
- **3B.2:** Strong coverage of police, NSA, prison, and pro-military tribal-force abuse and weak accountability. Gap: incomplete military–tribal command records and no comprehensive prosecution dataset.
- **3B.3:** Searched directly in English and Arabic. Sisi used exclusionary security rhetoric about the Muslim Brotherhood, while state-aligned media used dehumanizing smears. **Residual gap:** no verified 2022 personal Sisi call for hatred, revenge, or physical violence.
- **3B.4:** Mixed coverage: prosecution/complaint and women-protection structures existed, pardons and releases occurred, but judicial orders were reportedly ignored and investigations lacked independence. Gap: no audited complaint-to-disposition data.
- **3B.5:** Strong adverse coverage of terrorism charges, surveillance, emergency courts, protest controls, and administrative restrictions. Gap: incomplete case-level testing of whether any specific security use was necessary and proportionate.
- **3B.6:** Evidence supports a broad political fear climate alongside continuing Sinai insurgent violence. Gap: no representative fear survey or comparable state-versus-nonstate victimization series.
- **3B.7:** Mixed evidence: real women-protection programs and church-fire response, but continuing violence against women, refugee abuse, religious discrimination, and an alleged child execution. Gap: sparse group-specific outcome data, especially disability, migrant, LGBT, and regional coverage.
- **3B.8:** Strong adverse coverage of COP27 arrests, surveillance, association restrictions, and retaliation. Pardons and dialogue were limited contrary evidence.
- **3B.9:** Mixed: rapid high-level response to the church fire, but preventive protest repression and alleged Sinai executions show coercive crisis responses. Gap: no complete after-action records for spontaneous disturbances.
- **3B.10:** Partial only. Target-year evidence establishes ongoing state repression and insurgent casualties, while some protection programs expanded. **Residual gap:** no methodologically comparable inherited-versus-year-end series covering political, communal, criminal, and preventable insecurity, so net safety change remains unresolved.

Another general Chapter 3B search wave is unlikely to add much value: the main patterns are corroborated across NGO, UN, official, domestic, and specialist sources. ## Artifact Y

## Scope and orientation

This assessment covers calendar year 2022 only. Abdel Fattah el-Sisi was president throughout the period.

### Authority and practical control

Egypt’s constitution made the president head of state, supreme commander of the armed forces, chair of the National Defence Council, appointing authority for senior civil and military officials, and a necessary reviewing authority for military-court and emergency-court judgments. The 2019 amendments also strengthened presidential influence over senior judicial appointments. This supports strong attribution for presidential pardons, national security policy, emergency-law substitutes, senior appointments, and military policy. It does not establish that Sisi personally ordered every arrest, custodial abuse, or local police action.

Practical coercive power was distributed among the Interior Ministry, National Security Agency, General Intelligence Service, armed forces, military intelligence, prison administration, Supreme State Security Prosecution, and an executive-influenced judiciary. These were durable institutions with operational autonomy, but the evidence shows neither meaningful civilian independence from the presidency nor an effective institution capable of holding politically protected security personnel accountable.

### Inherited baseline

Sisi entered 2022 after nearly nine years of rule, not as a new ruler inheriting another government’s system. The relevant baseline was therefore largely his own accumulated record:

- mass political imprisonment and prolonged pretrial detention;
- recurring enforced disappearance and torture allegations;
- emergency courts and terrorism prosecutions used against peaceful critics;
- prisons largely closed to independent monitoring;
- a long-running North Sinai insurgency accompanied by displacement, demolition, civilian-harm allegations, and severe reporting restrictions;
- a public-order system in which peaceful protest had become rare because of arrest risk.

The nationwide state of emergency formally ended in October 2021, but contemporaneous legal reporting found that legislation enacted around its termination retained analogous presidential and military powers.

### Shocks and constraints

The continuing ISIS-Sinai insurgency was a real physical-security threat. The State Department counted at least 95 reported terrorist incidents and roughly 260 casualties in Sinai, while also finding a decline in terrorist activity compared with previous years. Its report expressly warned that casualty figures could not be confirmed because of restricted reporting. The Russia–Ukraine and currency shocks contributed to November protest calls, but do not excuse preventive arrests of peaceful critics and are not otherwise treated as chapter evidence.

### Information environment

Independent access to prisons and North Sinai was highly restricted. Domestic monitors operated under surveillance, prosecution, travel bans, asset freezes, and closure pressure. Complaint and arrest counts are documented cases, not prevalence estimates. Sparse reporting cannot be treated as evidence of safety.

The supplied local D12 prior says PTS, CIRIGHTS, V-Dem and UCDP all establish a severe national physical-integrity/repression context, but no underlying country-year rows were supplied, and I could not reliably extract exact 2022 values from their live interfaces. I therefore retain that prior as context, not as a fully extracted evidence record. UCDP’s thresholded one-sided-violence series also should not be treated as a complete measure of custodial deaths, disappearance, torture, or individual executions.

## Compact evidence index

- **WEB-3B-001 — Presidential coercive authority:** Establishes formal responsibility for national security policy, senior appointments, pardons, and exceptional-court review.
- **WEB-3B-002 — Broad physical-integrity abuse and impunity:** Cross-sector annual finding covering killings, disappearance, torture, detention, and ineffective accountability.
- **WEB-3B-003 — Hadhoud death and failed investigation:** Detailed 2022 test case of disappearance, possible torture, medical neglect, concealment, and rejected remedy.
- **WEB-3B-004 — Pardons accompanied by more arrests:** Personally attributable favorable initiative, sharply limited by continuing security-agency arrests.
- **WEB-3B-005 — Presidential pardons for convicted police officers:** Direct presidential action reportedly nullifying rare torture/death-in-custody convictions.
- **WEB-3B-006 — COP27 protest-call crackdown:** Nationwide preventive detention, terrorism accusations, surveillance, and temporary disappearance.
- **WEB-3B-007 — Torture-tainted mass trial:** Exemplary punishment through mass proceedings, death sentences, child abuse allegations, and denial of counsel.
- **WEB-3B-008 — Sinai executions by army-aligned forces:** Visual evidence implicating both military and aligned militias, with no reported investigation.
- **WEB-3B-009 — Reduced but continuing insurgent danger:** Genuine favorable security outcome, constrained by opaque casualty reporting and abusive counterterrorism methods.
- **WEB-3B-010 — Eritrean expulsions:** Collective refoulement, child detention, lack of asylum procedure, and exposure to disappearance or torture.
- **WEB-3B-011 — Women’s protection: enforcement and serious gaps:** Concrete FGM and harassment enforcement alongside failure to enact comprehensive violence legislation.
- **WEB-3B-012 — Religious-minority violence and mixed state response:** Identity killings persisted, but police and courts intervened in several individual cases.
- **WEB-3B-013 — LGBT targeting:** Police entrapment, forced examinations, criminalization and lack of protection against violence.
- **WEB-3B-014 — Dialogue and human-rights strategy:** Presidential reform initiatives provided releases and proposed reforms but lacked independent design and did not displace repression.
- **WEB-3B-015 — Sisi’s “people of evil” rhetoric:** Direct scapegoating language about the Muslim Brotherhood, but no located 2022 order or explicit exhortation to private violence.

## Disposition of the ten lenses

### 3B.1 — Protection from state violence

The strongest evidence is adverse. Annual monitoring found credible reports of arbitrary killing, disappearance, torture, life-threatening detention conditions, and political imprisonment, with only exceptional prosecutions (WEB-3B-002). Hadhoud’s case supplies a detailed institutional example (WEB-3B-003), while the mass trial demonstrates exemplary punishment and deficient due process (WEB-3B-007). Pardoned releases are genuine contrary evidence but were numerically and structurally limited (WEB-3B-004).

### 3B.2 — Abuse by coercive services and aligned actors

Police, National Security, prisons, military forces, and army-aligned militias were all implicated. Accountability was exceptional, and Sisi reportedly pardoned 13 convicted police officers in three custodial-death cases (WEB-3B-005). Verified Sinai footage implicated army and aligned militia members without a reported investigation (WEB-3B-008).

### 3B.3 — Personal incitement or dehumanization

Sisi publicly described the Muslim Brotherhood as “people of evil” and spoke of a “cursed alliance” of evil and destruction (WEB-3B-015). That rhetoric supported political scapegoating and blurred the distinction between armed threats and the wider opposition. However, this research did **not** locate a 2022 speech in which he explicitly ordered violence, revenge, or private attacks against a protected group or peaceful opponent. The personal-incitement finding should therefore remain narrower than the institutional-repression finding.

### 3B.4 — Due process, complaints, oversight, investigation

Formal complaint routes, prosecutors, courts, a human-rights strategy, pardon committee, and dialogue existed. In practice, prosecutors repeatedly failed to investigate security-force abuse; prisons lacked independent monitoring; emergency judgments lacked ordinary appeal; and politically protected actors enjoyed impunity (WEB-3B-002, 003, 005, 007, 014). Hadhoud’s family pursued prosecution and court review, but authorities withheld evidence and the appeal was rejected.

### 3B.5 — Emergency, terrorism, surveillance, and administrative controls

The formal emergency ended before 2022, but successor legislation preserved curfew, evacuation, military infrastructure-protection, and other exceptional powers. Terrorism accusations and surveillance were applied to suspected peaceful protest and online activity (WEB-3B-002, 006, 007, 013). This was not a pattern of narrow counterterrorism confined to armed actors.

### 3B.6 — Fear and insecurity

ISIS activity reportedly declined, a real favorable outcome (WEB-3B-009). But that improvement coexisted with nationwide fear of arrest, disappearance, surveillance, and punishment for dissent (WEB-3B-002, 006). Counterinsurgency also involved aligned-force executions (WEB-3B-008). The evidence therefore does not support treating reduced insurgent capacity as an unqualified reduction of domestic fear.

### 3B.7 — Women, children, minorities, and vulnerable groups

The record is mixed but predominantly inadequate:

- authorities prevented some FGM procedures and obtained convictions under strengthened law;
- police and courts responded to several individual sectarian killings;
- Eritrean adults and children were detained and expelled without individualized protection;
- violence against women remained widespread without comprehensive legislation;
- LGBT people faced state entrapment and abusive examinations;
- some Copts and refugees faced identity violence and barriers to protection.

See WEB-3B-010 through WEB-3B-013. Child defendants also appeared in the torture-tainted mass trial (WEB-3B-007).

### 3B.8 — Peaceful protest and organization

The November crackdown is direct evidence that authorities treated mere responsiveness to protest calls as grounds for preventive arrest and terrorism investigation. Nearly 700 detentions were documented across 18 governorates, including about 40 people whose whereabouts were initially unknown (WEB-3B-006). Civil-society investigations, bans, and retaliation also undermined the announced “year of civil society” (WEB-3B-014).

### 3B.9 — Crisis and flare-up response

Two different crisis responses matter. Against ISIS, authorities degraded the organization and investigated or prosecuted alleged attackers, but aligned forces committed apparent executions and reporting remained opaque (WEB-3B-008, 009). Against spontaneous protest calls, the response was preventive nationwide detention, surveillance, intimidation, and terrorism accusations rather than restrained crowd protection (WEB-3B-006).

### 3B.10 — Inherited-to-end safety

Compared with the beginning of 2022, ISIS attack frequency and severity reportedly declined. Some detainees were released and a limited number of vulnerable-group offenders were prosecuted. But the inherited repression apparatus remained intact: disappearance, torture, custodial deaths, political detention, protest suppression, and impunity continued, while new arrests exceeded releases in the documented comparison. There is no defensible evidence that residents as a whole ended 2022 safer from political violence and state coercion, though they may have faced a weakened insurgent threat in parts of Sinai.

## Source-state audit

### Fully extracted and accepted

The 15 WEB-3B records below.

### Opened corroboration

- **HRW, World Report 2023: Egypt:** corroborates continuing disappearance, torture, Hadhoud, Sinai executions, prison opacity, refugee expulsion, violence against women, and LGBT digital targeting. Some passages depend on the same HRW investigations used in WEB-3B-003 and WEB-3B-008.
- **USDOS, 2022 Country Report on Human Rights Practices:** independently synthesizes diplomatic reporting, NGOs, media and official material, but sometimes depends on the same local NGO incident datasets.
- **USDOS, 2022 International Religious Freedom Report:** corroborates identity killings and documents both police/court action and unresolved protection gaps.
- **CIHRS National Dialogue position paper:** corroborates that releases occurred but were regarded by domestic rights advocates as insufficient while imprisonment and arrests continued.
- **HRW World Report 2022:** establishes the inherited 2021 baseline, not a 2022 outcome.
- **OHCHR Hadhoud communication EGY 5/2022:** opened through the UN communications index, but the index page did not expose the complete communication text.

### Reused supplied sources

- E002 → WEB-3B-001, underlying constitution opened through an accessible official/constitutional reproduction.
- E003–E006 → WEB-3B-004 and WEB-3B-014; Amnesty source opened.
- E007 → WEB-3B-003; HRW/Amnesty joint investigation opened.
- E015 → WEB-3B-009; terrorism report opened.
- E036 → WEB-3B-006; HRW/ECRF source opened.
- E037 → corroboration for WEB-3B-014; CIHRS source opened.
- E046 → contextual prior only; no machine record because underlying indicator rows were not provided or reliably extracted.

### Promising but still requiring inspection

- PTS country-year raw data for Egypt, 2021 and 2022.
- CIRIGHTS Egypt country-year rows and coding notes for killing, disappearance, torture, and political imprisonment.
- V-Dem `v2x_clphy` Egypt observations and confidence intervals.
- UCDP GED and One-Sided Violence Egypt actor/event rows for 2022.
- Egyptian Public Prosecution case files for the police-officer pardons and Hadhoud investigation.
- Official pardon decrees or Gazette publication identifying the 13 police officers.
- Arabic local case reporting outside the source families already used, particularly from Sinai and Upper Egypt.
- National Council for Human Rights 2022 complaint and prison-visit records, if complete files become available.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia and Reddit results: discovery-only, unstable or user-generated.
- Generic economic-crisis reporting: rejected absent a direct physical-safety causal chain.
- Foreign-policy and overseas military material: outside Chapter 3B.
- Generic freedom or democracy scores without a specific physical-integrity nexus.
- The reported “65,000 political prisoners” figure: rejected as a precise prevalence estimate because methodology and denominator were not established.
- Municipal incidents lacking documented national policy, notice, command, or obstruction of remedy.
- UCDP nonappearance or sub-threshold cases as proof of safety: its organized-violence threshold does not capture much of the conduct at issue.

### Access blocked

- Two official State Information Service speech pages returned cache/502 failures when opened directly; their indexed text was preserved as a limited source for WEB-3B-015.
- The OHCHR Special Procedures communications search page returned an internal error on full opening.
- Exact PTS, CIRIGHTS, V-Dem and UCDP country-year rows were not exposed through accessible indexed pages.
- Some Egyptian official Facebook statements and linked leaked hospital records were unavailable or unsuitable as stable standalone records.

## Counts

Counts are by distinct URLs or dataset endpoints, not search-result appearances:

- Discovered: **39**
- Opened or substantively extracted: **22**
- Accepted into developed records: **15**
- Reused from supplied list: **11 provisional IDs consolidated into 7 accepted facts plus 1 context-only prior**
- Opened corroboration not given a separate fact record: **6**
- Rejected: **10**
- Access blocked: **6**
- Uninspected promising leads: **7**

Categories overlap where, for example, a reused source also served as an accepted source.

## Remaining questions and saturation judgment

Further research is most likely to add material information in four areas:

1. exact structured-indicator values and changes from 2021 to 2022;
2. official legal documentation of the police-officer pardons;
3. independent command-chain evidence for Sinai militias;
4. broader Arabic-language evidence on minority protection and actual remedies.

Additional general searching is unlikely to change the central findings: it mostly repeats the same detention, torture, impunity, protest, and Sinai evidence. Targeted archival or dataset work could improve precision and attribution, especially for 3B.3 and 3B.10.

# Félix Tshisekedi — 2B (Congo, The Democratic Republic of the)


## Artifact X

Chapter 2B research is complete for Félix Tshisekedi, DRC, 2022. No score was assigned, and no other chapter was researched.

The evidence presents a mixed record. Tshisekedi repeatedly participated in regional diplomacy and publicly placed diplomacy before war. Rwanda’s material support for M23 was independently corroborated, so the central external-threat claim was not manufactured. At the same time, FARDC elements collaborated with abusive armed groups, including FDLR-linked forces; state agents committed substantial violations; and the state of siege continued despite weak protection outcomes. Tshisekedi denounced proxy use, but enforcement was ineffective. Evidence on corrupt or patronage-driven military spending, personal diversionary motives, and his personal response to specific military failures remains insufficient.

## Reused evidence verified

Seven previously indexed source-claim units were reopened or independently rediscovered and retained without being counted as new documents:

- E006, Amnesty: the state of siege was repeatedly extended, transferred civil authority to military officials, exposed civilians to military courts, suppressed dissent, and did not achieve its protection rationale. This bears principally on 2B.4, 2B.9 and 2B.10. [Amnesty briefing](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2022/05/drc-one-year-on-state-of-siege/)
- E009, African Union: Tshisekedi supported the Nairobi and Luanda processes, regional dialogue, de-escalation and a regional-force component amid continuing atrocities. This supports 2B.1 and 2B.7 but is not proof of implementation. [AU communiqué](https://www.peaceau.org/en/article/communique-of-the-1103th-meeting-of-the-peace-and-security-council-held-on-31-august-2022-on-the-situation-in-the-eastern-democratic-republic-of-congo-drc)
- E026, Congo Research Group: UN findings supported RDF involvement with M23 and showed that this support preceded cited cross-border rocket incidents; the same evidence documented FARDC collaboration with armed groups. This both corroborates Tshisekedi’s external-threat case and qualifies his dismissal of FARDC-FDLR allegations. [CRG analysis](https://www.congoresearchgroup.org/en/2022/11/09/goe-report-why-knowing-the-facts-isnt-enough/)
- E054, Luanda communiqué: ceasefire, M23 withdrawal, cessation of support to all armed groups, disarmament, displaced-person return and monitoring were adopted, with force contemplated for noncompliance. [PA-X agreement record](https://www.peaceagreements.org/agreements/2466/)
- E055, Nairobi communiqué: the process included prisoner release, community consultations, P-DDRCS review, humanitarian access, child-soldier release and continued cessation of hostilities. [PA-X agreement record](https://www.peaceagreements.org/agreements/2465/)
- E057/E008, U.S. country report: state and nonstate forces committed grave abuses; practical civilian control was limited; some prosecutions and training occurred, but impunity remained substantial. [2022 country report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2089109.html)
- E045, Human Rights Watch: M23 committed attacks on civilians, while evidence placed RDF personnel in M23 camps; government soldiers and other armed groups also committed violence. [HRW report](https://www.hrw.org/news/2022/07/25/dr-congo-resurgent-m23-rebels-target-civilians)

## Lens coverage

- 2B.1: Strongly covered. Tshisekedi pursued Luanda, Nairobi, direct and mediated talks, while retaining military coercion as a fallback.
- 2B.2: Covered mainly by negative and contextual searches. No defensible evidence was found that Tshisekedi initiated annexation, cross-border conquest or an elective foreign war in 2022. Joint operations with Uganda and regional-force deployment were justified as responses to armed groups, but necessity and proportionality cannot be fully verified.
- 2B.3: Covered. M23/RDF and ADF threats were genuine; however, proxy cooperation and some expansionist-resource rhetoric complicate an entirely clean defensive narrative.
- 2B.4: Strongly covered adversely. FARDC violations, civilian casualties, abusive detention and state-of-siege practices were documented; limited convictions provide contrary evidence.
- 2B.5: Strongly covered. Tshisekedi condemned proxies and signed commitments to end support, but FARDC-linked collaboration continued.
- 2B.6: Covered with a residual gap. The principal allegation of Rwandan support was corroborated, but the November speech overstated the falsity of FARDC-FDLR allegations and asserted Rwandan mineral/expansion motives without equivalent evidence. No evidence established deliberate knowledge of falsehood.
- 2B.7: Strongly covered favorably but with implementation limits. Multiple ceasefires and peace processes were pursued, yet compliance was partial and fighting continued.
- 2B.8: Partially covered. Spending increased amid real threats. No defensible evidence established enrichment of Tshisekedi’s networks, rewards to security elites, intimidation of neighbors, or personal-strength projection.
- 2B.9: Moderately covered. Tshisekedi admitted FARDC had been surprised, condemned proxy use and reshuffled command; prosecutions occurred. Evidence does not establish personal acceptance of responsibility for systemic civilian harm or state-of-siege failure.
- 2B.10: Covered as an outcome lens. Regional relations and eastern security were worse by late 2022, but M23’s resurgence, independently documented RDF support, inherited armed conflict and weak institutions substantially constrain attribution.

Exact residual gaps are intent/knowledge under 2B.6; procurement beneficiaries and corruption under 2B.8; and ruler-level responsibility or corrective action for particular operational failures under 2B.9. Nationally complete civilian-harm and operational denominators also remain unavailable.

## Research-wave accounting

- Plausible documents discovered: 58
- Underlying documents/pages opened or fetch-attempted: 22
- Accepted: 15 source-claim units total
- Newly accepted: 8
- Reused and verified: 7
- Rejected: 33, principally duplicative reporting, wrong-period retrospective material, unsupported commentary, Wikipedia/Reddit summaries, or records unable to establish ruler attribution
- Access-blocked or materially incomplete: 7, including intermittent UN PDF cache failures, an Actualite.cd accountability page, a Radio Okapi node/audio record and a SIPRI fact-sheet timeout
- Distinct accepted organizations: 9
- Accepted source types: UN field/Secretary-General reporting, UN expert investigation, official diplomatic communiqué, presidential transcript, peace-agreement archive, international NGO reporting, local UN-supported media, local budget reporting and research-institute budget archive

Another broad Chapter 2B wave is unlikely to add substantial value. A narrowly targeted archival wave could still improve the three residual gaps by obtaining military procurement audits, cabinet or parliamentary oversight records, complete military-court dispositions, and internal follow-up to Tshisekedi’s anti-proxy order. Those materials are not readily available in the open source landscape inspected here.

The workspace was read-only and no shell/file-write facility was available, so `research-ledger-manifest.json` could not be updated. The eight `SOURCE_CLAIM_JSON` records above are the deterministic manifest additions; the prior cumulative ledger was not replaced or altered.

## Artifact Y

## Scope and orientation

Period assessed: **1 January–31 December 2022**. “2022–2022” is treated as a single calendar year. No score is assigned.

### Authority

Félix Tshisekedi was president throughout 2022. Constitutionally and practically he was the supreme commander of the armed forces and police, chaired the Higher Defence Council, appointed senior security officials, directed foreign policy, and controlled the presidential decrees sustaining the state of siege. A formal declaration of war nevertheless required cabinet deliberation, Defence Council advice, and parliamentary authorization. Parliament repeatedly approved two-week extensions of the state of siege, so responsibility was shared but presidentially initiated and sustained.

Tshisekedi directly participated in the Luanda interstate negotiations and authorized the Nairobi process, Ugandan operations on Congolese territory, and deployment of the East African Community Regional Force. Weak command systems, fragmented FARDC units, provincial military government, foreign partners, MONUSCO, and autonomous or semi-coordinated militias substantially limited practical control over individual operations.

### Baseline and conflict gate

The international-security exposure gate is decisively met. Tshisekedi inherited:

- Decades of regionalized warfare in eastern DRC, involving Rwanda, Uganda, Burundi, foreign armed groups, and numerous Congolese militias.
- A 2021 state of siege in North Kivu and Ituri.
- Operation Shujaa, the joint FARDC–Ugandan campaign against the ADF, begun in late 2021.
- M23’s reactivation in November 2021 and unresolved obligations arising from earlier peace arrangements.
- Extremely weak civilian protection and military accountability.

The local D13/UCDP prior records conflict events, fatalities, one-sided violence and government-actor killings but does not expose its numerical rows in the supplied material. An opened UCDP-authored publication provides the strongest accessible D13 fact: **at least 3,200 civilians were killed in one-sided violence in DRC during 2022**, while expressly warning that this excludes crossfire and indiscriminate shelling. UCDP classified Rwandan support to M23 against the Congolese government as one of five 2022 cases of state support to a rebel organization fighting another state.

The D14/SIPRI prior and SIPRI-derived series report approximately **US$371 million** in DRC military expenditure in 2022, about **21.2% above 2021**. This measures spending, not procurement integrity or ruler enrichment.

### Shocks and constraints

The major period shock was the sharp M23 offensive, including the capture of Bunagana and other territory, with increasingly strong evidence of direct Rwandan military intervention. The ADF, CODECO, Nyatura and other inherited threats continued simultaneously. Conflict displaced hundreds of thousands of people, while humanitarian agencies operated under severe access and funding constraints. The Ukraine-related food and energy shock added fiscal and humanitarian pressure but does not explain the core military decisions.

Reporting was comparatively plural but incomplete. MONUSCO, UN experts, NGOs, regional bodies and local journalists documented major incidents, yet military-site restrictions, insecurity, intimidation and inaccessible M23-held territory produced substantial undercounting and attribution uncertainty.

## Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-2B-001 | Constitutional and practical command | Establishes Tshisekedi’s authority and institutional constraints. |
| WEB-2B-002 | UCDP conflict burden | Establishes unusually severe conflict exposure and deterioration. |
| WEB-2B-003 | M23/Rwandan intervention | Corroborates a genuine external defensive threat, while recording contested shelling allegations. |
| WEB-2B-004 | Luanda ceasefire framework | Direct evidence that Tshisekedi accepted ceasefire, withdrawal, verification and dialogue. |
| WEB-2B-005 | Nairobi dialogue commitments | Shows political, humanitarian and DDR alternatives to force. |
| WEB-2B-006 | Partial implementation, continued fighting | Prevents treating signed commitments as a completed settlement. |
| WEB-2B-007 | FARDC collaboration with FDLR/armed groups | Central adverse proxy-control and lawful-security evidence. |
| WEB-2B-008 | State-force civilian harm | Documents FARDC killings and sexual violence, with partial accountability measures. |
| WEB-2B-009 | State-of-siege failure and abuse | Connects presidential emergency policy to repression and worsened protection outcomes. |
| WEB-2B-010 | Authorized foreign/regional forces | Shows reliance on Uganda, MONUSCO and EACRF against genuine threats, with control risks. |
| WEB-2B-011 | Public threat framing and restraint | Records substantially corroborated Rwanda claims, coercive rhetoric, diplomacy and an anti-stigmatization instruction. |
| WEB-2B-012 | Military expenditure increase | Establishes mobilization but not patronage or personal enrichment. |
| WEB-2B-013 | Year-end end state | Shows limited diplomatic progress but greater regionalization, displacement and insecurity. |

## Disposition of the ten lenses

### 2B.1 — Diplomacy versus force

**Mixed, with meaningful favorable evidence.** Tshisekedi joined the Luanda and Nairobi processes, signed the 23 November ceasefire framework, accepted monitoring and withdrawal arrangements, and supported DDR and humanitarian commitments (WEB-2B-004, 005). He also continued Uganda’s Operation Shujaa, sought a force-capable EAC deployment, strengthened FARDC “strike capacity,” and excluded direct M23 bargaining unless withdrawal conditions were met (WEB-2B-010, 011). The underlying M23/Rwandan and ADF threats were credible, so military resistance was not simply a war of choice.

### 2B.2 — War of choice, cross-border coercion or proxy conflict

**No persuasive evidence that Tshisekedi initiated an annexationist or external war of choice.** The 2022 M23/RDF offensive was directed against Congolese territory (WEB-2B-003). However, DRC authorized Ugandan forces to operate on its territory and FARDC elements cooperated with FDLR and other armed groups, extending regional proxy dynamics and creating foreseeable escalation risks (WEB-2B-007, 010). The available evidence does not establish that Tshisekedi personally ordered the FDLR cooperation.

### 2B.3 — Defensive need versus prestige or manufactured threats

**The central external threat was genuine, but security policy was not cleanly confined to lawful defense.** UN, EU, UCDP and independent reporting corroborated RDF support for M23 (WEB-2B-002, 003, 007). Tshisekedi’s description of Rwandan aggression therefore was not manufactured. Calling M23 “terrorists,” expanding military capacity, and relying on abusive militias nevertheless simplified a conflict containing unresolved political, communal and prior-agreement issues (WEB-2B-005, 007, 011). Evidence for diversionary or personal-prestige motivation is insufficient.

### 2B.4 — Civilian protection and humanitarian law

**Adverse overall, with limited remedial evidence.** Armed groups caused most documented abuses, but FARDC personnel committed extrajudicial killings and conflict-related sexual violence, while state-of-siege authorities killed protesters and subjected civilians to military courts (WEB-2B-008, 009). Some soldiers and police were convicted, and action plans and training were initiated, but impunity—especially at higher levels—remained pervasive. Evidence tying individual battlefield violations directly to Tshisekedi is generally absent.

### 2B.5 — Control of allies, proxies and arms recipients

**Serious adverse evidence.** UN findings and the EU’s year-end response identified FARDC cooperation with FDLR and other Congolese armed groups, despite their abusive records. Arms-trafficking charges against FARDC officers and abuses by allied Burundian actors reinforce the control problem (WEB-2B-007). The Luanda commitment to end all support to armed groups and some arrests count as contrary evidence, but implementation was incomplete. Direct presidential authorization of particular militia cooperation remains unproved.

### 2B.6 — Truthful public explanation

**The principal accusation was substantially corroborated, but official framing was selective.** Independent UN evidence supported the claim that Rwanda materially backed M23 and operated inside DRC (WEB-2B-003, 011). Official descriptions omitted or downplayed FARDC cooperation with FDLR, which provided Rwanda with a real—though not legally sufficient—security grievance (WEB-2B-007). There is no adequate evidence of fabricated intelligence directed by Tshisekedi.

### 2B.7 — Ceasefires, talks and reconciliation

**Favorable at the level of presidential commitment; weak and incomplete in outcome.** Tshisekedi signed or accepted the Luanda ceasefire, bilateral normalization, monitoring, withdrawal, cantonment, refugee-return and cessation-of-support provisions. The Nairobi dialogue added prisoner release, child-soldier release, humanitarian access, consultations and P-DDRCS review (WEB-2B-004, 005). M23 withdrew from Kibumba, but continued hostilities and incomplete compliance showed that the process had not produced peace by year’s end (WEB-2B-006).

### 2B.8 — Military spending and patronage

**Real security need is established; patronage conclusion is not.** SIPRI-derived data indicate spending rose to about US$371 million, 21.2% above 2021, during an exceptionally severe conflict year (WEB-2B-002, 012). Weak payroll and procurement controls make diversion plausible, but the searched 2022 material does not establish that the increase enriched Tshisekedi, his family, or a specified military network. This lens remains low-confidence.

### 2B.9 — Accountability and correction

**Partial institutional accountability, little ruler-level accountability.** Authorities convicted some soldiers, police and intelligence personnel and arrested officers accused of supplying CODECO; joint UN action plans and training were adopted (WEB-2B-007, 008). But the government repeatedly extended a demonstrably ineffective state of siege, did not provide an effective public remedy for its structural abuses, and higher-level impunity persisted (WEB-2B-009). No evidence was found of Tshisekedi accepting personal responsibility for failed protection or FARDC–FDLR cooperation.

### 2B.10 — End-state change

**Relations and security were worse at the end of 2022 than at its beginning, despite credible diplomacy.** The year ended with M23 holding territory, direct Rwandan involvement, expanded regional-force deployments, severed ambassadorial relations, mass displacement and continued fighting. Luanda and Nairobi created a lawful diplomatic architecture and produced one partial withdrawal, but not a durable settlement (WEB-2B-004, 005, 006, 013). Much deterioration was caused by external and inherited actors rather than initiated by Tshisekedi.

## Source-state audit

### Opened independent corroboration

- UN Secretary-General/MONUSCO reports S/2022/503 and related 2022 reporting corroborate the multiple-actor threat, joint Ugandan operations, FARDC violations and limited prosecutions.
- Human Rights Watch’s M23 investigation independently corroborates M23 civilian killings and reviews UN photographic/aerial evidence of RDF personnel.
- The EU’s 31 December statement independently accepts the UN experts’ findings concerning both RDF/M23 support and FARDC–FDLR cooperation.
- UCDP’s peer-reviewed 2022 review independently classifies Rwandan support to M23 and quantifies DRC one-sided violence.
- The U.S. country report is secondary corroboration substantially dependent on UNJHRO, UN experts and NGO reporting; it is not counted as independent where it repeats those sources.

### Reused supplied sources, opened before use

E006, E008/E057, E009, E010, E025/E056, E026, E045, E054 and E055 were reconsidered. E054, E055, E006, E045 and E057 were opened and used. E025/E056 refer to the same Kenya Ministry of Defence article and were deduplicated. E008 and E057 are the same U.S. report and were deduplicated.

### Promising leads still requiring inspection

- Full French/English pagination of S/2022/967, especially annexes identifying individual FARDC units, commanders, dates and supplied weapons.
- National Assembly extension votes and Defence Committee reports for responsibility shared between the presidency and legislature.
- Audited 2022 defence appropriations, procurement contracts, payroll and off-budget security spending.
- Full Operation Shujaa agreement, rules of engagement and civilian-casualty investigations.
- Presidential correspondence or orders addressing FARDC–FDLR cooperation after UN findings.
- Implementation records for Nairobi prisoner-release, humanitarian-access and P-DDRCS commitments.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia conflict chronologies: useful for discovery but superseded by UN, PA-X, official and NGO records.
- Macrotrends and Helgi as final SIPRI authorities: they reproduce SIPRI data but are not the underlying database.
- Rwanda’s later UNSC statements: useful contrary advocacy but outside the target year and not independent evidence of 2022 events.
- 2024–2026 reporting on later Wazalendo structures: potentially retrospective, but too remote to establish Tshisekedi’s 2022 knowledge or orders.
- Government white-paper allegations not independently verified were not used as proof of individual events.

### Access-blocked or technically incomplete

- Local D13/D14 underlying files: the workspace supplied only the earlier summary, and no shell/file-reading interface was available in this research session.
- UN S/2022/967 PDF: search extraction exposed key text, but the direct UN PDF open returned an internal error.
- African Union communiqué URL: direct open was rejected by the retrieval service’s URL-safety layer.
- UN Secretary-General 25 November statement: direct open returned a cache miss.
- Leganet constitution page and SIPRI 2022 fact-sheet landing page: direct retrieval failed or timed out; search extraction and independent official/academic records were used cautiously.

### Counts

Counting unique underlying publications rather than duplicate URLs:

- Sources/leads discovered: **38**
- Successfully opened or substantively extracted: **21**
- Accepted as evidence or independent corroboration: **17**
- Supplied sources reused after opening: **9 provisional IDs**, representing **7 unique publications**
- Rejected as final evidence: **7**
- Access-blocked or technically incomplete: **5**
- Uninspected promising leads retained: **6**

## Remaining research value

Further searching is most likely to add material information on:

1. Who authorized FARDC–FDLR cooperation and when the presidency learned of it.
2. Rules of engagement and civilian-harm reviews for Operation Shujaa and FARDC artillery.
3. Defence procurement, payroll diversion and patronage.
4. Implementation of prisoner-release, humanitarian and DDR commitments.
5. Ruler-level acceptance or avoidance of accountability.

The factual landscape for the conflict baseline, RDF/M23 intervention, peace-process commitments, state-of-siege abuses and year-end deterioration is comparatively saturated. The military-patronage and ruler-direction questions are not.

Research-wave accounting:

- Documents discovered or screened: 43 plausible records.
- Underlying documents opened or directly inspected: 24.
- Accepted source-claim units: 14, of which 12 are final evidence and 2 context.
- Rejected after inspection or screening: 14, chiefly duplicates, post-period material, Wikipedia/Reddit summaries, weak snippets, or records that established capability without the relevant behavior.
- Access-blocked or materially incomplete: 5. These included the SIPRI chapter page timeout, the IISS page returning no text, the NIDS PDF fetch failure, an Iran/IAEA mirror cache miss, and intermittent extraction failure for the NNSA PDF. The NNSA search index and extracted report metadata still supported a context-only unit.
- Newly discovered evidence units: 8.
- Reused and reverified evidence units: 6, corresponding principally to prior leads E001/E002/E034/E035/E036/E040/E042. Reused documents were not counted as newly discovered.
- Distinct source organizations: 10 — Chinese MFA, Chinese UN Mission, Chinese Embassy in the US, Chinese NNSA, SIPRI, US Department of Defense, Congressional Research Service, United Nations, Ifri, The Guardian, The Irish Times, and TIME, counting closely related Chinese diplomatic publishers as one broader source family gives 10.
- Source types: official diplomatic statements, joint intergovernmental declaration, regulatory annual report, UN meeting record, external-government assessment, legislative research report, independent institute/academic analysis, and contemporaneous journalism.

Lens coverage:

- 1B.1: P5 declaration, Xi’s Ukraine statement, NFU advocacy, arsenal expansion.
- 1B.2: Strong coverage of responsible public rhetoric; no verified Xi nuclear threat was found in 2022.
- 1B.3: Declaratory unauthorized-use safeguards and civil regulatory institutions, counterbalanced by possible launch-on-warning movement. Residual gap: military custody, authentication, accident and near-miss data remain secret.
- 1B.4: P5/NPT/IAEA commitments are documented. Residual gap: no major binding bilateral arms-control or inspection agreement concluded by China in 2022, and implementation evidence is limited.
- 1B.5: Taiwan exercises, coercion, strategic-deterrence integration and suspension of dialogue are covered. Residual gap: no evidence found of an explicit nuclear threat used to shield the exercises.
- 1B.6: DPRK envoy diplomacy and the sanctions veto are covered with competing interpretations. Residual gap: no reliable evidence isolates Xi’s personal decision, private pressure on Pyongyang, or enforcement intensity at the Chinese border.
- 1B.7: Centralized authority, loyalty incentives, weakened feedback, and strategic-dialogue reluctance are documented. Residual gap: nuclear-specific adviser selection, expertise, internal dissent and actual decision procedures are opaque.
- 1B.8: Xi’s Ukraine de-escalatory rhetoric contrasts with the Taiwan exercises and closure of crisis channels.
- 1B.9: Military-AI safeguards and IAEA/nuclear-safety policy are documented, counterbalanced by opaque military-civil fusion. Residual gap: operational compliance, cyber-nuclear safeguards, biological implementation, and auditability are not independently visible.
- 1B.10: The year ended with stronger declared safeguards and some responsible rhetoric, but a materially larger, more diverse and potentially higher-readiness force and weaker crisis communication. This is evidence characterization, not a score.

A further Chapter 1B search wave would have limited but non-zero value. It might recover inaccessible NIDS/IISS material, full NNSA page locators, or archival PLA/CMC records. It is unlikely to close the central gaps—military custody, internal advice, Xi-specific launch authority, readiness, cyber-nuclear entanglement, and private pressure on North Korea—because those are products of state secrecy rather than insufficient ordinary web searching.

The workspace was read-only and no writable attempt directory was exposed, so `research-ledger-manifest.json` could not be updated. The cumulative merge payload for this turn is the 14 `SOURCE_CLAIM_JSON` records above; the parent ledger should append them without replacing prior entries.

## Artifact Y

## Scope and bottom line

Period resolved as **1 January–31 December 2022**; the user’s “2022–2022” is treated as a single target year.

**Exposure classification: `material_exposure`.** Xi Jinping chaired the Chinese Communist Party, the state, and the Central Military Commission (CMC). Public scholarship indicates that alerting or launching Chinese nuclear forces required agreement at the highest party-military level, while Xi’s October 2022 report directed implementation of the “CMC chairman responsibility system,” expansion of “strategic deterrence,” and modernization of joint command, warning, cyber, and intelligent warfare capabilities.

The record is mixed but not symmetrical:

- China maintained its longstanding declaratory no-first-use (NFU) policy, joined the January P5 risk-reduction statement, promoted safeguards and nuclear-security cooperation, and Xi personally opposed nuclear use and threats amid Russia’s war.
- China did not issue direct nuclear threats in the Taiwan crisis.
- Against that, China was undertaking the largest expansion in its nuclear history, building more than 300 apparent missile silos and increasing readiness, diversity, and potential launch-on-warning capability. Its operational stockpile exceeded 400 warheads by the Pentagon’s 2022 estimate.
- During the August Taiwan crisis, China conducted unprecedented coercive exercises and suspended senior military and maritime-safety dialogue—the channels most relevant to controlling accidental escalation.
- China vetoed strengthened UN measures against North Korea after renewed ICBM testing, although it argued that sanctions were counterproductive and harmful to diplomacy and civilians.
- Formal nuclear-safety and military-AI rules were precautionary, but public evidence does not independently establish implementation inside the opaque nuclear command system.

This dossier describes evidence and does **not** assign a score.

## Orientation

### Authority

Xi’s personal authority was exceptionally broad. He was CCP general secretary and CMC chairman throughout 2022, and president of the PRC. His Party Congress report explicitly required stronger implementation of the CMC-chairman responsibility system and a stronger strategic-deterrence system. Independent analysis assessed that major nuclear-force changes were highly unlikely without Xi’s authorization, although operational records and specific orders remain secret.

Attribution is therefore strongest for Xi’s direct speeches, Party-Congress directives, leadership-level diplomacy, and broad force-development policy. It is weaker for technical implementation, individual PLA deployments, regulatory enforcement, and ambassadorial statements.

### Inherited baseline

China entered 2022 with:

- a longstanding official NFU doctrine and negative security assurances;
- a historically small, centrally controlled, low-readiness arsenal, with many warheads normally separated from launchers;
- an arsenal already undergoing rapid transformation since approximately 2019;
- three large apparent ICBM-silo fields already under construction;
- the NPT, IAEA safeguards, an unratified CTBT signature, a nuclear-safety statute effective since 2018, and participation in P5 risk-reduction diplomacy.

The silo expansion therefore was not initiated wholly inside calendar 2022, but Xi’s government maintained and developed it during the target year.

### Shocks and constraints

The central shocks were Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and nuclear threats; North Korean missile and nuclear developments; deteriorating US-China strategic relations; and the August Taiwan crisis after Nancy Pelosi’s visit. These created unusually discriminating opportunities for restraint, crisis communication, proliferation policy, and nuclear rhetoric.

### Information environment

China publishes doctrine and legal instruments but not a warhead total, fissile-material production data, alert records, nuclear exercises, incident reports, or detailed command arrangements. Independent assessment consequently relies heavily on satellite imagery and external intelligence estimates. Absence of public accidents, dissent, or nuclear threats is not proof of safety or restraint.

The workspace shell was unavailable, so the requested local researcher guide and local D17/FAS files could not be opened. Supplied local facts were used only after their underlying web sources were opened wherever accessible.

## Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-1B-001 | Xi’s concentrated nuclear and military authority | Establishes practical attribution for doctrine and major force changes. |
| WEB-1B-002 | Inherited NFU, centralized custody, low readiness | Provides the inherited risk-control baseline. |
| WEB-1B-003 | More than 300 new apparent missile silos | Strong evidence of rapid quantitative and qualitative expansion. |
| WEB-1B-004 | Stockpile exceeded 400 operational warheads | Shows force growth during Xi’s tenure and target-year posture. |
| WEB-1B-005 | China joined the January P5 risk statement | Favorable formal commitment on nuclear war, arms races, and unauthorized use. |
| WEB-1B-006 | China reaffirmed NFU and risk reduction at the NPT | Favorable doctrine and diplomacy, limited by lack of verification and force expansion. |
| WEB-1B-007 | Nuclear Safety Law framework | Institutional evidence on custody, regulation, expertise, emergency response, and transparency. |
| WEB-1B-008 | Xi opposed nuclear use and threats over Ukraine | Direct ruler-attributed responsible rhetoric in a live nuclear crisis. |
| WEB-1B-009 | Taiwan exercises plus suspension of safety dialogue | Adverse crisis-management and coercion evidence. |
| WEB-1B-010 | China vetoed additional DPRK sanctions | Adverse or disputed proliferation response involving a client-dependent state. |
| WEB-1B-011 | Military-AI precautionary position | Favorable formal dual-use governance principles, without verified implementation. |
| WEB-1B-012 | Xi ordered stronger deterrence, warning, cyber and intelligent forces | Links risk institutions with simultaneous acceleration of strategic capability. |
| WEB-1B-013 | CTBT cooperation without ratification | Mixed arms-control record: monitoring support but no binding ratification. |
| WEB-1B-014 | China supported IAEA safeguards and peaceful-use cooperation | Favorable multilateral position, principally declaratory. |

## Disposition by lens

1. **1B.1 — Risk reduction versus leverage:** Mixed, with substantial adverse evidence. WEB-1B-005, 006 and 008 show risk-reduction commitments; WEB-1B-003, 004 and 012 show rapid expansion. WEB-1B-009 shows conventional coercion and withdrawal of guardrails during crisis.

2. **1B.2 — Nuclear rhetoric:** Substantially favorable in the observable record. China retained NFU, joined the P5 statement, and Xi opposed nuclear use and threats (WEB-1B-005, 006, 008). No direct Chinese nuclear-use threat was found in 2022. This does not resolve whether nuclear capability was implicitly supporting Taiwan coercion.

3. **1B.3 — Command, custody and accident prevention:** Mixed and opaque. Centralized civilian authorization, separated warheads, and the statutory safety framework are favorable (WEB-1B-002, 007). Increasing readiness, possible launch-on-warning development, entanglement with conventional systems, and secrecy create countervailing risks (WEB-1B-003, 004, 012).

4. **1B.4 — Arms control and inspection:** Mixed. China supported the NPT, IAEA safeguards, CTBT objectives, fissile-material negotiations, and P5 dialogue (WEB-1B-005, 006, 013, 014). It had not ratified the CTBT, rejected the TPNW approach, did not enter a binding arsenal-control arrangement, and expanded its forces.

5. **1B.5 — Nuclear shield for conventional coercion:** Important adverse possibility, not conclusively proven. The Taiwan exercises and strategic-deterrence buildup coincide (WEB-1B-009, 012), and independent scholarship identified a possible “nuclear shield” for non-nuclear escalation. China did not explicitly threaten nuclear use, and there is insufficient evidence that Xi ordered the exercises under nuclear cover.

6. **1B.6 — Proliferation by allies or clients:** Adverse/disputed. China vetoed additional DPRK sanctions after ICBM tests (WEB-1B-010). China argued sanctions would worsen humanitarian conditions and reduce diplomatic prospects; the record does not establish Chinese assistance to the DPRK nuclear program in 2022.

7. **1B.7 — Expertise and institutions:** Limited favorable institutional evidence. The Nuclear Safety Law mandates qualified specialists, independent regulation, training, research, inspections, and emergency capacity (WEB-1B-007); Xi called for strengthened risk-monitoring and technical systems (WEB-1B-012). No sufficiently independent evidence was found about nuclear advisers’ professional autonomy versus political loyalty.

8. **1B.8 — Crisis behavior and channels:** Mixed but materially adverse in the Taiwan crisis. Xi’s Ukraine rhetoric was de-escalatory (WEB-1B-008), but Beijing suspended senior military and maritime-security dialogue while conducting unprecedented Taiwan exercises (WEB-1B-009).

9. **1B.9 — Other catastrophic technologies:** Formally favorable but implementation-uncertain. China’s military-AI paper required human control, suspension capability, accountability, lifecycle safeguards, and restraint against premature deployment (WEB-1B-011). Xi ordered stronger cyber, biological, nuclear, data, warning, and emergency systems while also accelerating intelligent and cyber warfare capability (WEB-1B-012). No independently verifiable 2022 outcome evidence was found for biological or frontier-AI safeguards.

10. **1B.10 — End-of-period posture:** More dangerous in capability and crisis-interaction terms than the inherited Xi-era baseline, despite retained doctrinal restraints. Force size, silo capacity, readiness options, and Taiwan escalation all increased; NFU, centralized authorization, safety law, and responsible rhetoric remained mitigating features (WEB-1B-002–009, 012).

## Source-state accounting

### Opened corroboration

- Arms Control Association’s 2023 synthesis corroborates centralized control, NFU continuity, low inherited readiness, force expansion, and the uncertainty surrounding Xi’s motives.
- SIPRI independently corroborates the silo buildout and broad expansion.
- Pentagon reporting independently estimates more than 400 operational warheads, but is an adversarial-government source and depends partly on classified intelligence.
- Reuters independently documents the Taiwan exercises and suspension of dialogue.
- UN meeting records corroborate the DPRK veto and record both China’s justification and other members’ objections.
- CTBTO records corroborate Chinese monitoring-station cooperation and continued non-ratification.

### Reused supplied sources, opened before use

E001, E002, E034, E035, E036, E039, E040, E042, E046 and E048. E003 was not reused because the supplied Carnegie URL concerned South Asia and did not cleanly support the China-specific claim as described. E037, E041, E044, E045 and E047 remain useful leads but were not needed for a distinct accepted record here.

### Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full 2022 Pentagon China Military Power Report PDF, especially its nuclear command, launch-on-warning, and fissile-material sections.
- FAS *Chinese Nuclear Weapons, 2023* / later Nuclear Notebook tables for precise end-2022 estimates.
- Tong Zhao’s October 2022 APLN paper on Russian coercive signaling lessons.
- Carnegie’s 2022 strategic-stability study on conventional/nuclear/cyber entanglement.
- IAEA annual safeguards and nuclear-security implementation records specific to China.
- Chinese-language CMC regulations governing Rocket Force custody, authentication, and accident reporting.
- Personnel records for 2022 nuclear command, CAEA, NNSA, and Rocket Force appointments.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia P5 statement: rejected because the MFA primary text was available.
- Supplied Carnegie South Asia URL (E003): rejected for claim/URL mismatch.
- Reddit summaries: rejected as derivative and unnecessary.
- Later 2024–26 Chinese white papers: rejected as primary evidence for 2022, except as possible retrospective leads.
- Search snippets alleging implementation without a directly inspectable underlying record: rejected.

### Access blocked or technically incomplete

- Local researcher guide and D17/FAS workspace files: shell interface unavailable.
- UN press page for the DPRK vote timed out on direct open; its search extraction and separate UN records were available.
- Full Pentagon PDF was discovered but not fully extracted in this pass; the official release and official detailed summary were opened.

## Remaining research value

Further research is most likely to add material information to lenses **1B.3, 1B.5, 1B.7, and 1B.9**—especially launch-on-warning implementation, nuclear-conventional entanglement in the Taiwan theater, appointment criteria and professional autonomy, and actual AI/biosecurity enforcement. Open-source searching is otherwise approaching repetition. Classified or Chinese internal records would be far more discriminating than additional general commentary.
